Updated items given the new choice of
\mathbb{C}
in Example
8

7
Example
3
continued.

The data scientist team may be interested in computing the causal effect of taking both stages of the drug on the recovery rate of the disease,
Q=P(Y_{A=1,B=1}=1)
. However,
\mathcal{M}_{L}
is not observed, and instead, the observational data from
P(\mathbf{V}_{L})
(
\mathbb{Z}=\{P(\mathbf{V}_{L})\}
) and the C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
from Fig.
7(b)
are given. Is
Q

\tau
-ID from
\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
? It turns out the answer is no. To witness, consider the following pair of models.

\mathcal{M}_{1}=\!\begin{cases}\mathbf{U}_{1}=\{U_{XY},U_{Y0},U_{Y1}\}\\
\mathbf{V}_{H}=\{X,Y\}\\
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\begin{cases}&X\leftarrow f^{1}_{X}(u_{XY})=u_{XY}\\
&Y\leftarrow f^{1}_{Y}(x,u_{XY},u_{Y0},u_{Y1})\\
&=\begin{cases}x\vee u_{Y0}&u_{XY}=0\\
u_{Y1}&u_{XY}=1\end{cases}\end{cases}\\
P(\mathbf{U}_{1})=\begin{cases}P(U_{XY}=1)=0.34\\
P(U_{Y0}=1)=0.1\\
P(U_{Y1}=1)=0.852941\end{cases}\end{cases}

(40)

\mathcal{M}_{2}=\!\begin{cases}\mathbf{U}_{2}=\{U_{XY},U_{Y0},U_{Y1}\}\\
\mathbf{V}_{H}=\{X,Y\}\\
\mathcal{F}_{2}=\begin{cases}&X\leftarrow f^{2}_{X}(u_{XY})=u_{XY}\\
&Y\leftarrow f^{2}_{Y}(x,u_{XY},u_{Y0},u_{Y1})\\
&=\begin{cases}\neg x\wedge u_{Y0}&u_{XY}=0\\
u_{Y1}&u_{XY}=1\end{cases}\end{cases}\\
P(\mathbf{U}_{2})=\begin{cases}P(U_{XY}=1)=0.34\\
P(U_{Y0}=1)=0.1\\
P(U_{Y1}=1)=0.852941\end{cases}\end{cases}

(41)

Every value of
P^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(\mathbf{V}_{L})
can be computed using the table in Fig.
3
, producing the table in Fig.
7
. The corresponding values of
\tau(P^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(\mathbf{V}_{L}))
can then be computed as shown in Fig.
7(c)
via Eq.
10
. One can verify that both
P^{\mathcal{M}_{1}}(\mathbf{V}_{H})
and
P^{\mathcal{M}_{2}}(\mathbf{V}_{H})
match the values in Fig.
7(c)
, implying that both
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
are
P(\mathbf{V}_{L})
-
\tau
consistent with
\mathcal{M}_{L}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
also induce
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
from Fig.
7(b)
:
f_{Y}
takes
X
as input, and
f_{X}
and
f_{Y}
share
U_{XY}
as a confounding variable. Also,
f_{X}
does not contain
Y
as an input.

However, computing
\tau(Q)=P(Y_{X=1}=1)
leads to two different answers, i.e.:

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{M}_{1}}(Y_{X=1}=1)

\displaystyle=P(U_{XY}=0)+P(U_{XY}=1,U_{Y1}=1)

\displaystyle=0.66+(0.34)(0.852941)=0.95,

(42)

and

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{M}_{2}}(Y_{X=1}=1)

\displaystyle=P(U_{XY}=1,U_{Y1}=1)

\displaystyle=(0.34)(0.852941)=0.29.

(43)

These values are not only different, but also neither are equal to the true value
P^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(Y_{A=1,B=1}=1)=0.5
, as computed in Eq.
16
. A scientist using model
\mathcal{M}_{1}
may conclude that the treatment is extremely effective, while a scientist using model
\mathcal{M}_{2}
may conclude the opposite: the treatment is not only ineffective, it is even harmful. The query is not
\tau
-ID in this case, and no further inferences of this query should be made at this stage.

\blacksquare

Now consider the following
\tau
-ID example.

8
Example
3
continued.

The data scientist team is studying causal inference and notes that Example
7
showed that the query
Q=P(Y_{A=1,B=1}=1)
is non-ID from the available data. However, suppose instead, a different set of clusters is used that includes
R
, i.e.
\mathbb{C}=\{\mathbf{C}_{1}=\{A,B\},\mathbf{C}_{2}=\{Y\},\mathbf{C}_{3}=\{R\}\}
. The domain of
R
would remain the same, so
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{3}}(R)=R
. Constructing
\tau
with these clusters, the high level variables are revised to
\mathbf{V}_{H}=\{R,X,Y\}
, and
\tau(P^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(\mathbf{V}_{L}))
can be computed as shown in the table in Fig.
8(b)
. The C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
would be updated to the one in Fig.
8(a)
.

It turns out that now,
Q=P(Y_{A=1,B=1}=1)
is
\tau
-ID, and this can be shown by applying the backdoor-criterion
(
Pearl 2000
, Thm. 3.3.2)
, adjusting over the variable
R
as follows.

\displaystyle P(Y_{A=1,B=1}=1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r}P(Y=1\mid r,A=1,B=1)P(r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r}P(Y=1\mid r,X=1)P(r).

(44)

Then
\sum_{r}P(Y=1\mid r,X=1)P(r)
could immediately be computed from
P(\mathbf{V}_{H})
.

As an example, consider the following choice of
\mathcal{M}_{H}
, designed in a systematic way to match
\tau(P^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(\mathbf{V}_{L}))
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{H}

\displaystyle=\{U_{RY},U_{X0},U_{X1},U_{Y0},U_{Y1},U_{Y2},U_{Y3}\}

(45)

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{H}

\displaystyle=\{R,X,Y\}

(46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{H}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}&R\leftarrow f_{R}(u_{RY})=u_{RY}\\
&X\leftarrow f_{X}(r,u_{X0},u_{X1})=\begin{cases}u_{X0}&r=0\\
u_{X1}&r=1\end{cases}\\
&Y\leftarrow f_{Y}(x,u_{RY},u_{Y0},u_{Y1},u_{Y2},u_{Y3})\\
&=\begin{cases}u_{Y0}&x=0,u_{RY}=0\\
u_{Y1}&x=0,u_{RY}=1\\
u_{Y2}&x=1,u_{RY}=0\\
u_{Y3}&x=1,u_{RY}=1\end{cases}\end{cases}

(47)

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{U}_{H})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}P(U_{RY}=1)&=0.5\\
P(U_{X0}=1)&=0.04\\
P(U_{X1}=1)&=0.64\\
P(U_{Y0}=1)&=0.1\\
P(U_{Y1}=1)&=0.1\\
P(U_{Y2}=1)&=0.1\\
P(U_{Y3}=1)&=0.9\end{cases}

(48)

One can verify that, indeed,
P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(\mathbf{V}_{H})
matches
\tau(P^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(\mathbf{V}_{L}))
from Fig.
8(b)
. It is also clear that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
induces
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Further, we can compute the query to find that

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(Y_{X=1}=1)

\displaystyle=P(U_{RY}=0,U_{Y2}=1)+P(U_{RY}=1,U_{Y3}=1)

\displaystyle=(0.5)(0.1)+(0.5)(0.9)

\displaystyle=0.5,

(49)

which matches the true value
P^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(Y_{A=1,B=1}=1)=0.5
from Eq.
16
.

\blacksquare

The definition of
\tau
-ID provides rigorous semantics to answer whether a query can be inferred across abstractions. The next step is to establish an approach to determine
\tau
-ID when given the available data and graph. For this purpose, one fundamental result is that the notion of
\tau
-ID is actually equivalent to classical identification in the higher level space, as shown by the following proposition.

1
Dual Abstract ID.

Consider a counterfactual query
Q
over
\mathbf{V}_{L}
, a constructive abstraction function
\tau
w.r.t. clusters
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
, a C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and data
\mathbb{Z}
from
\mathbf{V}_{L}
.
Q
is
\tau
-ID from
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathbb{Z}
if and only if
\tau(Q)
is ID from
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\tau(\mathbb{Z})
.

\blacksquare

In words,
\tau
-ID and classical ID on the high level space are equivalent. This is a powerful result since it implies that inferences can be made about the low level space by using existing results in the high level space. Our goal is to learn a higher level SCM
\mathcal{M}_{H}
to make inferences about
\mathcal{M}_{L}
, and we build on the machinery of Neural Causal Models (NCMs)
(
Xia et al. 2021
)
toward this goal. NCMs allow one to take the graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
as an inductive bias (a
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-NCM as described in Def.
4
) and leverage gradient-based methods to fit any SCM within the constrained space. Indeed, identification in NCMs can be shown to be equivalent to classical identification when considering models of the same granularity
(
Xia, Pan, and Bareinboim 2023
, Thm. 3)
. When combined with Thm.
1
, this implies the following result.

1
Abstract ID with NCMs.

Q
is
\tau
-ID from
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathbb{Z}
iff
\tau(Q)
is Neural-ID from
\widehat{\Omega}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}})
and
\tau(\mathbb{Z})
. Moreover, if it is ID, then
Q
can be computed by
\tau(Q)
from any
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-NCM
\widehat{M}
that is
\tau(\mathbb{Z})
-consistent.

\blacksquare

In words, determining
\tau
-ID is equivalent to determining neural identification (identification in the space of NCMs) on the higher level space. Further, to evaluate
Q
in identifiable cases,
\tau(Q)
can be queried from any
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-NCM
\widehat{M}
that is
\tau(\mathbb{Z})
-consistent. Corol.
1
implies that we can perform causal identification and estimation across abstractions using the NeuralID algorithm
(
Xia, Pan, and Bareinboim 2023
, Alg. 1)
on the high level space. This procedure is shown in Alg.
2
. First,
\tau
is constructed as described in Def.
6
given the clusters. Then, a
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-NCM is constructed over high-level variables
\mathbf{V}_{H}
. Two parameterizations of the NCM are created. Both are optimized to fit the transformed data
\tau(\mathbb{Z})
, but one is optimized to maximize the transformed query
\tau(Q)
while the other is optimized to minimize it. If both parameterizations return the same result, then it must be the true value of the query; otherwise, the query is not identifiable.

To implement this algorithm in practice, we leverage the GAN-NCM approach introduced in
Xia, Pan, and Bareinboim 2023
; see details in Appendix
C
. Alg.
2
is sound and complete for solving the abstract identification problem, as shown below.

2
Soundness and Completeness.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{L}
be the low-level SCM,
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
be inter/intravariable clusters of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
,
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
be a C-DAG,
Q
be a query, and
\widehat{Q}
be the result from running Alg.
2
with inputs
\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{M}_{L})>0
,
\mathbb{C}
,
\mathbb{D}
,
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and
Q
. Then,
Q
is
\tau
-ID from
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathbb{Z}
if and only if
\widehat{Q}
is not
FAIL
. Moreover, if
\widehat{Q}
is not
FAIL
, then
\widehat{Q}=Q(\mathcal{M}_{L})
.

\blacksquare

While Alg.
2
solves the abstract ID problem, the consequences of the results in this section are more general. Notably, if
Q
is indeed
\tau
-ID (which can be verified through Alg.
2
), the algorithm produces a neural model
\widehat{M}
that serves as a proxy SCM that is
Q
-
\tau
consistent with the true model
\mathcal{M}_{L}
. Such an SCM could serve as a generative model of the distribution
Q
, which has many uses. The samples generated from such a model could be used to estimate the query, or, in more complex settings such as with image data, it may be desirable to simply have novel generated samples consistent with the causal invariances embedded in the system.

1

2

Input :
query
Q
,
\mathcal{L}_{2}
datasets
\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{M}_{L})
, C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and admissible inter/intravariable clusters
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
satisfying invariance condition

Output :

Q(\mathcal{M}_{L})
if identifiable,
FAIL
otherwise.

\mathbf{V}_{H}\leftarrow\mathbb{C},\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{H}}\leftarrow\mathbb{D}

3

\tau\leftarrow\textnormal{{AbsFunc}}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{D})

//
from Def.
6

\textnormal{{$\widehat{M}$}}\leftarrow\textnormal{{NCM(}}\textnormal{\emph{{{$\mathbf{V}_{H}$}}, {{$\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}$}} }}\textnormal{{)}}

//

\textnormal{{$\bm{\theta}_{\min}^{*}$}}\!\leftarrow\!\arg\min_{\bm{\theta}}\tau(Q)(\textnormal{{$\widehat{M}$}}(\bm{\theta}))
s.t.

4

s.t.

Algorithm 2

NeuralAbstractID
– Identifying and estimating queries across abstractions using NCMs.

4
Representations in Learning Abstractions

In many applications, the choice of intervariable clusters
\mathbb{C}
is natural and can be made in tandem when deciding the assumptions of the C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
11
11

11

Please refer to App.
D.1
for best practices on how to choose or learn intervariable clusters when they are not given.
. On the other hand, fully specifying the intravariable clusters
\mathbb{D}
is usually challenging when working with high-dimensional data like images. If an intervariable cluster contained three binary variables from
\mathbf{V}_{L}
, then specifying its intravariable cluster would require specifying some partition over its eight values,
(0,0,0),(0,0,1),\dots,(1,1,1)
, which is not difficult. However, if an intervariable cluster contained, for example,
128\times 128
pixels, each with 256 possible values, then the size of the domain of this cluster would be
256^{128\times 128}
. Specifying an arbitrary partition over this many values is infeasible, as doing so would require an enumeration of every possible image along with some label designating each one to a cluster. In this section, we investigate the problem of learning abstractions when the intravariable clusters
\mathbb{D}
are left unspecified.

While coarser clusters tend to be better in practice due to the dimensionality reduction, the theory in this paper can be applied for any choice of
\mathbb{D}
so long as the AIC (Def.
8
) holds. Hence, a possible constraint when learning
\mathbb{D}
is to find a set of clusters such that the AIC is not violated. To this effect, the following result can be leveraged.

5
.

Consider a low level SCM
\mathcal{M}_{L}
and constructive abstraction function
\tau
w.r.t. clusters
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
.
\mathcal{M}_{L}
is guaranteed to satisfy the AIC w.r.t.
\tau
if and only if
\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}=\{\{\mathbf{c}_{i}\}:\mathbf{c}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\}
for all
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.

\blacksquare

In words, the AIC is satisfied when the intravariable clusters are maximal (i.e. each value is assigned to its own cluster). Consequently, this means that Alg.
2
can be applied in any case where
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
is a bijective mapping between
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{V_{H,i}}
. Also implied by this result is that, without additional information, one cannot choose any coarser clustering without potentially violating the AIC
12
12

12

In many cases, there may be additional information in the form of invariances (e.g. rotational invariance in image data). In such cases, this information can be leveraged to learn coarser clusters. See Appendix
D.3
for more details.
. For some intuition on why this is the case, consider the following example.

9
Example
1
continued.

Consider once again the nutrition example where carbohydrates (
C
), fat (
F
), and protein (
P
) were combined to form the high-level variable, calories (
Z
). Calories was defined to be
Z=4C+9F+4P
, resulting in a set of intravariable clusters where
(c_{1},f_{1},p_{1})
and
(c_{2},f_{2},p_{2})
were clustered together if
4c_{1}+9f_{1}+4p_{1}=4c_{2}+9f_{2}+4p_{2}
.

It turns out that, without additional information, it is possible that this choice of clustering violates the AIC. For example, it could be the case that in the true model
\mathcal{M}_{L}
, the function for BMI
f_{B}
depends heavily on protein, since muscle density may affect BMI more than fat. In an extreme example, suppose

B\leftarrow f_{B}(c,f,p,u_{B})=p+u_{B},

(50)

that is,
f_{B}
only depends on protein out of the three macronutrients. In this case,
(C=0,F=0,P=20)
and
(C=20,F=0,P=0)
would result in different values of
B
despite being clustered together in the same intravarable cluster, therefore, violating the AIC. Without information about
f_{B}
, it is not known whether any given two tuples,
(c_{1},f_{1},p_{1})
and
(c_{2},f_{2},p_{2})
, would witness this violation, so the only option is to leave all values of
(C,F,P)
in their own intravariable clusters.

\blacksquare

This example illustrates that without further information about the functions of the underlying SCM, it is, in general, impossible to cluster two intravariable values together without potentially violating the AIC. This is also the case with the cholesterol discussion in Ex.
4
and
5
. Without knowing
f_{Y}
, it would be impossible to determine whether the cluster choice in Ex.
5
would work better than the one in Ex.
4
.

Still, Prop.
5
states that the intravariable clustering which leaves every value in its own cluster will always satsify the AIC. While this choice of
\mathbb{D}
does not reduce the dimensionality of the abstracted space, this means that we are not restricted to the original space of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
and can choose any
\mathbf{V}_{H}
with the same cardinality. In practice, this means that we can choose the option for
\mathbf{V}_{H}
that is the most beneficial for a given task. For example, some choices of
\mathbf{V}_{H}
and corresponding domains
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{H}}
may have desirable properties such as simpler gradient computation, disentangled variables, or compatibility with arithmetic operations. In order to leverage this insight, we introduce the representational NCM.

11
Representational NCM (RNCM).

A representational NCM (RNCM) is a tuple
\langle\widehat{\tau},\widehat{M}\rangle
, where
\widehat{\tau}(\mathbf{v}_{L};\bm{\theta}_{\tau})
is a function parameterized by
\bm{\theta}_{\tau}
mapping from
\mathbf{V}_{L}
to
\mathbf{V}_{H}
, and
\widehat{M}
is an NCM defined over
\mathbf{V}_{H}
. A
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-constrained RNCM (
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-RNCM) is an RNCM
\langle\widehat{\tau},\widehat{M}\rangle
such that
\widehat{\tau}
is composed of subfunctions
\widehat{\tau}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
for each
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
(each with its own parameters
\bm{\theta}_{\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}}
), and
\widehat{M}
is a
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-NCM (Def.
4
).

\blacksquare

Figure 9:
Example using graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
shown in (a) to compare (b) the
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-NCM and (c)
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-RNCM. Functions of the NCM directly output values of the lower level variables (grouped by clusters in
\mathbb{C}
), while functions of the RNCM output values of their higher level counterparts, mapped by
\widehat{\tau}
.

In words, an RNCM is a pair of a parameterized abstraction function
\widehat{\tau}
and an NCM
\widehat{M}
defined over the space of high level variables
\mathbf{V}_{H}
obtained from
\widehat{\tau}(\mathbf{V}_{L})
. The
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-RNCM is simply an RNCM constrained over the C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
, where
\widehat{\tau}
must use the intervariable clusters specified by
\mathbb{C}
. Fig.
9
illustrates the difference between a
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-RNCM and a standard
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-NCM. Given
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
in (a), the
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-NCM (b) directly defines the domains of the inputs and outputs of the functions to be the space of the variables
\mathbf{V}_{L}
. On the other hand, the
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-RNCM (c) defines the domains over
\mathbf{V}_{H}=\widehat{\tau}(\mathbf{V}_{L})
instead.

Training can be done as a two-step process, where first
\widehat{\tau}
is trained to map to an optimal task-specific space, and then
\widehat{M}
can be trained on
\widehat{\tau}(\mathbf{V}_{L})
(e.g., through Alg.
2
). In the first step,
\widehat{\tau}
(parameterized by
\theta_{\tau}
) can be trained according to a loss such as

\displaystyle L_{\widehat{\tau}}(\mathbf{v}_{L})

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\widehat{\tau}^{-1}(\widehat{\tau}(\mathbf{v}_{L},\theta_{\tau});\theta_{\tau^{-1}})-\mathbf{v}_{L}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{r}L_{r}(\mathbf{v}_{L}),

(51)

where
\widehat{\tau}^{-1}
is a neural network parameterized by
\theta_{\tau^{-1}}
that attempts to invert
\widehat{\tau}
and recover the original
\mathbf{v}_{L}
,
L_{r}
is a separate representation regularizer, and
\lambda_{r}
is its regularization strength. The first term in Eq.
51
is used to enforce bijectivity between
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{V_{H,i}}
, as required by Prop.
5
. In words, one can train
\widehat{\tau}
in an autoencoder-like setup
(
Kramer 1991
;
Kingma and Welling 2014
)
with a reconstruction loss. The second term is left open-ended and can be used to impose a desired form on the output of
\widehat{\tau}
. The following example helps to ground this point.

10
.

Consider a simple example with
\mathbf{V}_{L}=\mathbf{X}\cup\{Y\}
, where
\mathbf{X}
is the collection of pixels representing an image of a cat or dog, and
Y
is a binary label (caused by
\mathbf{X}
) predicting whether the animal depicted in
\mathbf{X}
is aggressive or docile. Suppose researchers are studying the relationship between
\mathbf{X}
and
Y
and would like to work in a more abstract space. Denote
X_{H}
as the high-level counterpart of
\mathbf{X}
. Moreover, suppose the researchers are given another set of labels of
\mathbf{X}
, say
Z
, which state whether the animal in the image is a cat or dog.
Z
is not included in the study with
\mathbf{V}_{L}
, but it could potentially be used for learning the space of
X_{H}
.

Specifically, the researchers would like to train
\widehat{\tau}_{X}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{X_{H}}
such that the AIC is satisfied from Prop.
5
, and
X_{H}
additionally encodes some information about
Z
. One clever approach is to introduce another neural network
g(x_{H};\theta_{g})
parameterized by
\theta_{g}
that aims to classify
Z
from
X_{H}
. The researchers could train
\widehat{\tau}_{X}
with the loss
L_{\widehat{\tau}}
from Eq.
51
such that
L_{r}
is the classification loss of
g
. This would result in
X_{H}
encoding both enough information to reconstruct
\mathbf{X}
and also to classify
Z
. Although
X_{H}
may not have lower dimensionality than the original
X
, it may be more useful in a downstream task, such as building a classifier for
Y
(i.e.
X_{H}
may be a more well-behaved set of features for a classifier of
Y
than the original set of pixel values
\mathbf{X}
).

\blacksquare

In general,
\widehat{\tau}
can be thought of as a function mapping to a representation space, where the second term in Eq.
51
can be used to regularize the representation space for a desired task. The flexibility of this approach makes it amenable to the wide developments of the representation learning literature
(
Bengio, Courville, and Vincent 2013
)
. We empirically demonstrate this approach below in the experiment of Sec.
5.2
.

5
Experiments

In this section, we empirically evaluate the effects of utilizing abstractions in causal inference tasks. More details on the data-generating models and architectures can be found in Appendix
C
. Implementation code is publicly available at
https://github.com/CausalAILab/NeuralCausalAbstractions
.

5.1
Nutritional Study

We follow up on the nutrition study discussed in Ex.
1
. Since a BMI of 25 or over is considered overweight, the goal is to identify and estimate the query
Q=P(B_{D=d}\geq 25)
, the causal effect of diet on weight, given the available graphical constraints and observational data
P(\mathbf{V}_{L})
using Alg.
2
.
R
and
D
are 32-dimensional one-hot vectors, and the others are real-valued, so the query may be difficult to answer given such high-dimensional variables. Instead, it may be more effective to work in an abstract space with the proposed intervariable clusters
\mathbb{C}=\{D_{H}=\{D\},Z=\{C,F,P\},B_{H}=\{B\}\}
. The original graph
\mathcal{G}
and corresponding C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
are shown in Fig.
5
. We are also given intravariable clusters
\mathbb{D}
such that all values of
D_{H}
,
Z
, and
B_{H}
are clustered into binary categories. Specifically,
D_{H}=1
denotes unhealthy dishes,
Z=1
denotes high calorie count, and
B_{H}=1
denotes an overweight BMI (
\geq 25
).

We compare the effectiveness identifying and estimating
Q
with NCMs in three different settings, with results shown in Fig.
10
. The first approach (red) attempts to solve the problem directly in the space of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
by identifying and estimating
Q
from the original causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
and observational dataset from
P(\mathbf{V}_{L})
. The second approach (yellow) solves the same task but first normalizes each variable
13
13

13

This normalization approach is equivalent to using a constructive abstraction function
\tau
over the full set of clusters
\mathbb{C}=\mathbf{V}_{L}
and
\mathbb{D}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}
(with a bit of abuse of notation). That is, each variable and value are placed in their own cluster, and all values are simply remapped to different values.
of the data between 0 and 1. The third approach (blue) is the newly proposed approach and leverages the concept of
\tau
-ID, identifying and estimates
Q
from the C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
and high level data
\tau(P(\mathbf{V}_{L}))
. The model is trained over the abstract space of
\mathbf{V}_{H}
computed using the constructive abstraction function
\tau
defined on
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
. All three approaches are implemented in the style of GAN-NCM
(
Xia, Pan, and Bareinboim 2023
)
. Since
Q
is identifiable, the gap between the max and min queries computed in Alg.
2
are expected to be as small as possible. As shown in Fig.
10(a)
, the proposed approach (blue) converges quickly while others fail to close the gap between the max and min queries. Fig.
10(b)
also shows that the proposed approach can estimate
Q
with significantly lower error. Furthermore, since the proposed approach uses the C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
instead of the original causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
, the approach operates under fewer assumptions of domain knowledge.

(a)
Gaps between max and min query across 1000 training iterations when running Alg.
2
.

(b)
Mean absolute error (MAE) v. dataset size (in log-log scale) for query estimation.

Figure 10:
Results of the nutrition experiment. Our approach (blue) is compared with a GAN-NCM trained on raw data (red) and one trained on normalized data (yellow).

C
D
I

(a)
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
for Colored MNIST.

(b)
Image samples. Digits are highly correlated with the corresponding gradient color.

Figure 11:
Colored MNIST Experimental Setup

5.2
Colored MNIST Digits

Figure 12:
Colored MNIST results. Samples from various causal queries (top) are collected from competing approaches (left), with the ground truth samples from the data generating model shown in the bottom row. The left column simply shows image samples from
P(I)
from each of the models, while the second, third, and fourth columns show samples generated from an
\mathcal{L}_{1}
,
\mathcal{L}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{3}
query, respectively.

We evaluate the RNCM in a high-dimensional image dataset of colorized MNIST
(
Deng 2012
)
digits. Each image (
I
) has a corresponding digit (
D
) and color (
C
) label, and their relationships are shown in the C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
in Fig.
11(a)
. Color and digit are highly correlated (e.g. 0s are typically red, while 5s are cyan), as shown in Fig.
11(b)
. We evaluate three approaches in the task of sampling images from causal queries. The first approach is a naïve conditional GAN that does not take causality into account. The second is a standard GAN-NCM as described in
Xia, Pan, and Bareinboim 2023
. The third is called a GAN-RNCM, a GAN implementation of the representational NCM following the approach described in Sec.
4
.

Samples of the results are shown in Fig.
12
. All models are capable of producing digit images, as shown in the first column. The second column illustrates
P(I\mid D=0)
, the images conditioned on digit
=0
. Many red 0s are expected since most 0s are red in the dataset. The third column illustrates the interventional query
P(I_{D=0})
, the images with digits forced to be
0
through intervention. As interventions ignore the spurious correlations between color and digit, 0s of all colors are expected. Finally, the fourth column illustrates the counterfactual query
P(I_{D=0}\mid D=5)
, indicating what the digits would have looked like had they been 0, given that they were originally 5. Since 5s tend to be cyan, the samples are expected to be 0s that retain the cyan color of the 5s.

In all cases, GAN-RNCM (new approach) produces results closer to the expected outcomes, as shown by the ground truth. The conditional GAN fails to distinguish causal queries from conditional queries, and samples appear similar in the 2nd, 3rd, and 4th columns.
The standard GAN-NCM faces challenges disentangling color from digit, as shown from the presence of several non-zero digits in the 3rd column and several digits that resemble 5s in the 4th column.
Further, both the conditional GAN and the GAN-NCM face challenges in capturing the relationship between color and image in more complex distributions, as evident from the mosaic coloring in many of the samples.
The GAN-RNCM significantly outperforms the other approaches in terms of sample quality for the causal queries.

Conclusions

Figure 13:
Summary table of contributions of each section in terms of input assumptions and outputs. A checkmark (✓) indicates that the corresponding information is assumed to be available in that section. A gray checkmark indicates that it is implied by a stronger assumption (e.g. data
\mathbb{Z}
can be sampled from
\mathcal{M}_{L}
if
\mathcal{M}_{L}
is available). A dash (-) indicates that the information is not assumed. An approach that has fewer checkmarks makes fewer assumptions and is therefore more applicable in practice.

In this paper, we developed a new framework of abstractions based on the PCH’s layers with the goal of learning a high-level causal model at a coarser granularity.
In each section of the paper, we relaxed certain assumptions that are not easily achievable in practice, and showed how to obtain the high-level model under these particular settings.
These increasingly more refined results are summarized in Fig.
13
.

We started by noting that previous works on causal abstractions, such as
Beckers and Halpern 2019
, set a strong foundation of defining abstractions in rigorous terms. (A detailed discussion is provided in Appendix
B
.)
The main drawback is that these definitions are declarative, meaning that given a low level SCM
\mathcal{M}_{L}
, high level SCM
\mathcal{M}_{H}
, and an abstraction function
\tau
, the definitions in these works can be used to decide whether
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is an abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
or not.
Still, solving this decision task requires a substantial amount of input information, as highlighted in the first row of Fig.
13
, and which is unavailable in many practical settings.

We then examined in Sec.
2
the challenge of obtaining the abstract model
\mathcal{M}_{H}
when the same is unavailable, as shown in the second row of Fig.
13
.
We provided a systematic way of constructing
\tau
given inter/intravariable clusters (Defs.
5
and
6
).
Building on this, we developed Alg.
1
, which allows one to obtain the higher level abstraction
\mathcal{M}_{H}
given
\mathcal{M}_{L}
and the constructive abstraction function
\tau
.

Still, these results can be hard to apply since
\mathcal{M}_{L}
is not commonly available in some real world settings. Rather, partial information about
\mathcal{M}_{L}
through its data distributions may be available. We then described in Sec.
3
how to obtain
\mathcal{M}_{H}
under these restrictions, as shown in the third row of Fig.
13
.
Causal inferences on higher levels of the PCH cannot be realized using lower layers alone, as shown by Prop.
4
, which means that assumptions are needed.
The assumptions considered in this paper take the form of C-DAGs (Def.
9
), an abstract version of causal diagrams leveraging the intervariable clusters. Then, given the data
\mathbb{Z}
, the C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and the abstraction function
\tau
constructed from the clusters, Alg.
2
can be used to learn the abstract NCM
\widehat{M}_{H}
. Then,
\widehat{M}_{H}
can be used for tasks such as identification, estimation, or sampling.

We showed in Sec.
4
how to learn
\tau
, noting that acquiring intravariable clusters
\mathbb{D}
may be challenging in practice.
The fourth row of Fig.
13
highlight this task.
Specifically, we introduced the representational NCM or RNCM (Def.
11
), which parameterizes
\widehat{\tau}
as a neural network.
We then developed a learning procedure for intravariable clusters, considering task-specific objectives.
Whenever additional information about the problem is available in the form of invariances (e.g., translational, rotational, permutation), coarser clusters can be learned, as elaborated in Appendix
D.3
.

Finally, although not shown in the table, additional technical content can be found in Appendix
D
. Specifically, Appendix
D.1
discusses how to make the best choice of intervariable clusters
\mathbb{C}
when it is not given. Appendix
D.2
describes possible ways to relax the AIC (Def.
8
), which is an assumption that is made throughout the paper. We encourage further research on the topics covered in this paper, such as on the best way to learn
\widehat{\tau}
in an RNCM.

Appendix A
Proofs

A.1
Counterfactual Axioms and Properties

The proofs in this work will rely on the following counterfactual axioms from
Galles and Pearl 1998
and
Halpern 1998
:

1
(
Galles and Pearl 1998
, Thms. 1,2)
.

The following properties hold in SCMs:

1

(Composition). For any two singleton variables
Y
and
W
, and any set of variables
\mathbf{X}
in a causal model, we have

W_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{u})=w\Longrightarrow Y_{\mathbf{x},w}(\mathbf{u})=Y_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{u})

2

(Effectiveness) For all variables
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{W}
,
\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{x}
.

\blacksquare

As a consequence of these axioms, we can prove the following result:

3
.

For any set of variables
\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{X}
, we have

\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)},\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}[\mathbf{x}]}^{(2)}(\mathbf{u})}(\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}[\mathbf{x}]}(\mathbf{u})}(\mathbf{u}),

where
\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}=\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}\cap\mathbf{X}
,
\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}
are its corresponding values of
\mathbf{x}
, and
\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{(2)}=\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}\setminus\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}
.

\blacksquare

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{u})

(52)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)},\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}[\mathbf{x}]}^{(2)}(\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{u})

(53)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)},\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}[\mathbf{x}]}^{(2)}(\mathbf{u})}(\mathbf{u})

(54)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}[\mathbf{x}]}^{(1)}(\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}[\mathbf{x}]}^{(2)}(\mathbf{u})}(\mathbf{u})

(55)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}[\mathbf{x}]}(\mathbf{u})}(\mathbf{u}).

(56)

Here,
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
denotes the values of
\mathbf{x}
corresponding to
\mathbf{X}\setminus\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}
. Eq.
52
holds by definition. Eq.
53
holds by the composition property, which can be applied iteratively to each variable in
\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{(2)}
. Eq.
54
holds because the value of
\mathbf{Y}
is deterministic once
\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{Y}}
and
\mathbf{U}
, the inputs to its functions, are fixed. Eq.
55
holds by the effectiveness property. Finally Eq.
56
holds by definition.
∎

We also leverage the following results from
Correa, Lee, and Bareinboim 2021
:

12
(
Correa, Lee, and Bareinboim 2021
, Def. 3)
.

The set of (counterfactual) ancestors of
Y_{\mathbf{x}}
w.r.t. graph
\mathcal{G}
, denoted
An(Y_{\mathbf{x}})
, consist of each
W_{\mathbf{z}}
such that
W\in An(Y)_{\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X}}}
(which includes
Y
itself), and
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{x}\cap An(W)_{G_{\overline{\mathbf{X}}}}
.

\blacksquare

2
(
Correa, Lee, and Bareinboim 2021
, Thm. 1)
.

Let
\mathbf{W}_{*}
be an ancestral set, that is,
An(\mathbf{W}_{*})=\mathbf{W}_{*}
, and let
\mathbf{w}_{*}
be a vector with a value for each variable in
\mathbf{W}_{*}
. Then,

P(\mathbf{W}_{*}=\mathbf{w}_{*})=P\left(\bigwedge_{W_{\mathbf{t}}\in\mathbf{W}_{*}}W_{\mathbf{pa}_{W}}=w\right),

where each
w
is
w_{t}
and
\mathbf{pa}_{W}
is determined for each
W_{t}\in\mathbf{W}_{*}
as follows:

(i)

the values for variables in
\mathbf{Pa}_{W}\cap\mathbf{T}
are the same as in
\mathbf{t}
, and

(ii)

the values for variables in
\mathbf{Pa}_{W}\setminus\mathbf{T}
are taken from
\mathbf{w}_{*}
, corresponding to the parents of
W
.

\blacksquare

For the proofs in this work, we leverage a key concept of counterfactuals which we define as functional counterfactuals.

13
Functional Counterfactuals.

For any SCM
\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\rangle
, denote

\mathbf{F}=\left\{V_{i[\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}}^{(j)}]}:V_{i}\in\mathbf{V},\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}}\right\}

(57)

as the functional counterfactual set of
\mathcal{M}
, a set of counterfactual variables containing each variable intervened on every possible instantiation of its parents. Denote
\mathbf{f}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{F}}
as its instantiation and domain respectively
14
14

14

We intentionally use the notation of
\mathbf{F}
because this set of counterfactual quantities is heavily related to the functions of the SCM,
\mathcal{F}
. Notably, the behavior of a function
f_{V_{i}}\in\mathcal{F}
w.r.t.
\mathbf{U}
can be specified fully by the joint counterfactual
P\left(\bigwedge_{\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}}^{(j)}}V_{i[\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}}^{(j)}]}\right)
. This property is leveraged in the proof of Lem.
2
.
. If
\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}=\emptyset
, then
V_{i}\in\mathbf{F}
with no intervention.

\blacksquare

The idea behind this definition is to establish a standard family of counterfactual quantities which generalizes all other counterfactuals. We will see the power of functional counterfactuals through the following lemmas.

1
Functional Counterfactual Uniqueness.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\rangle
be an SCM with functional counterfactual set
\mathbf{F}
. Let
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{f})\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}}
be the set of values of
\mathbf{U}
such that for every
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{f})
, we have
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f}
when evaluating
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{u}
. Then, for any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}}
, there exists a unique
\mathbf{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{F}}
such that
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{f})
.

\blacksquare

Proof.

Note that for any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}}
, we can construct the value of
\mathbf{f}
such that
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{f})
as follows. For every
\mathbf{V}_{i}\in\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}}
, choose
v_{i}^{(j)}=f_{V_{i}}(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}}^{(j)},\mathbf{u})
. Collectively, these values of
v_{i}^{(j)}
can be used to form
\mathbf{f}
. It is clear that
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{f})
because
V_{i[\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}}^{(j)}]}(\mathbf{u})=f_{V_{i}}(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}}^{(j)},\mathbf{u})=v_{i}^{(j)}
for all
i
and
j
, implying that
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f}
. Further,
\mathbf{f}
is unique, since
V_{i[\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}}^{(j)}]}(\mathbf{u})
can only be equal to one unique deterministic value once
\mathbf{u}
is fixed.
∎

2
Functional Counterfactual Completeness.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\rangle
,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\langle\mathbf{U}^{\prime},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},P(\mathbf{U}^{\prime})\rangle
be two SCMs both defined over
\mathbf{V}
and with the same functional counterfactual set
\mathbf{F}
. Then
P^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f})=P^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f})
for all
\mathbf{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{F}}
if and only if
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
.

\blacksquare

Proof.

The backward direction of this proof is trivial since all functional counterfactuals belong to the set of all counterfactuals (i.e.
P^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f})\in\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M})
and
P^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f})\in\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
for all
\mathbf{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{F}}
). Therefore,
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
implies
P^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f})=P^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f})
for all
\mathbf{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{F}}
.

To prove the forward direction, we must show that
P^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f})=P^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f})
for all
\mathbf{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{F}}
implies
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
. Consider any arbitrary
\mathcal{L}_{3}
query from
\mathcal{M}
,

Q=P^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{y}_{1[\mathbf{x}_{1}]},\mathbf{y}_{2[\mathbf{x}_{2}]},\dots)\in\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}).

Denote
Q^{\prime}
as the equivalent value from
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. Denote
\mathbf{Y}_{*}
as the set of all of the counterfactual terms of
Q
. For each term
\mathbf{y}_{i[\mathbf{x}_{i}]}
, consider its ancestral set
An(\mathbf{y}_{i[\mathbf{x}_{i}]})
, and denote
An(\mathbf{Y}_{*})
as the union of all of these sets. Then note that

Q=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in{An(\mathbf{Y}_{*})\setminus\mathbf{Y}_{*}}}P^{\mathcal{M}}(An(\mathbf{y}_{1[\mathbf{x}_{1}]}),An(\mathbf{y}_{2[\mathbf{x}_{2}]}),\dots),

that is,
Q
is equal to the joint distribution of its ancestral set with all of the terms not in the original query marginalized away. Then, by Fact
2
, we have

Q=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in An(\mathbf{Y}_{*})\setminus\mathbf{Y}_{*}}P^{\mathcal{M}}\left(\bigwedge_{W_{\mathbf{t}}\in An(\mathbf{Y}_{*})}W_{\mathbf{pa}_{W}}=w\right),

(58)

where
w
and
\mathbf{pa}_{w}
are defined as specified in Fact
2
. This ancestral set factorization leaves every term with a single variable under the intervention of its parents. If there are duplicate terms (i.e.
W_{\mathbf{pa}_{W}}
appears multiple times for the same
W
and
\mathbf{pa}_{W}
), then either they can be reduced to a single term if every value of
w
is equal (since
p\wedge p=p
for any proposition
p
), or, if
w
is not equal for every term, then
Q=0
for both
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
since
W_{\mathbf{pa}_{W}}
cannot be equal to two values at once.

Finally, we note that the probability term is simply a marginalized quantity from the functional counterfactual, so we see that

Q=\sum_{a\in An(\mathbf{Y}_{*})\setminus\mathbf{Y}_{*}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{f}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{F}\setminus\mathbf{W}_{*}}P^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f})\right),

where
\mathbf{W}_{*}
refers to the set of
\mathbf{W}_{\mathbf{pa}_{W}}
terms in Eq.
58
, and
\mathbf{f}
is defined for each value of
V_{\mathbf{pa}_{V}}\in\mathbf{F}
as
w
if
V_{\mathbf{pa}_{V}}\in\mathbf{W}_{*}
or the value from
\mathbf{f}^{\prime}
otherwise.

Therefore, since
P^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f})=P^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{f})
, this implies that
Q=Q^{\prime}
. With this being true for all values of
Q\in\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M})
, this means that
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
.
∎

A.2
Proofs of Sec.
2

The abstractions in this work follow the theory developed by
Beckers and Halpern 2019
. We first note that
Beckers and Halpern 2019
utilizes the idea of “allowed interventions”. Specifically, for an SCM
\mathcal{M}_{L}
over variables
\mathbf{V}_{L}
, the notation
\mathcal{I}_{L}
is used to indicate a set of interventions on
\mathbf{V}_{L}
that are “allowed” in
\mathcal{M}_{L}
. This is relevant when defining interventions across abstractions, since not all interventions on the lower level will have a corresponding intervention on the higher level, as will be shown. Further, the notation
\mathcal{I}_{L}^{*}
is used to define the set of all possible interventions over
\mathbf{V}_{L}
.

We use the following definitions, translated to use the notation in our work
15
15

15

Note that there can be at most one such possible intervention
\mathbf{X}_{H}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{H}
such that
\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})=\mathbf{X}_{H}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{H}
. It is possible that no such intervention exists, but for this work, we only consider cases where there
\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})
exists for all
\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L}\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
.
.

14
(
D
)
ef. 3.12.

beckers2019abstracting]
Some relevant notation is defined as follows:

•

Given a set of variables
\mathbf{V}
,
\mathbf{X}\subseteq\mathbf{V}
, and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}}
, let
\rst(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{x})=\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}}:\mathbf{v}\text{ is consistent with }\mathbf{x}\}
.

•

Given variables
\mathbf{V}_{L}
and
\mathbf{V}_{H}
, mapping
\tau:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{H}}
, and value set
\mathbf{T}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}
, denote
\tau(\mathbf{T})=\{\tau(\mathbf{v}_{L}):\mathbf{v}_{L}\in\mathbf{T}\}
.

•

Given allowed interventions
\mathcal{I}_{L}
and
\mathcal{I}_{H}
over
\mathbf{V}_{L}
and
\mathbf{V}_{H}
respectively, define
\omega_{\tau}:\mathcal{I}_{L}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{H}
such that
\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})=\mathbf{X}_{H}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{H}
, where
\tau(\rst(\mathbf{V}_{L},\mathbf{x}_{L}))=\rst(\mathbf{V}_{H},\mathbf{x}_{H})
.

15
\tau
-Abstraction
(
Beckers and Halpern 2019
, Def. 3.13)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{L}=\langle\mathbf{U}_{L},\mathbf{V}_{L},\mathcal{F}_{L},P(\mathbf{U}_{L})\rangle
and
\mathcal{M}_{H}=\langle\mathbf{U}_{H},\mathbf{V}_{H},\mathcal{F}_{H},P(\mathbf{U}_{H})\rangle
be two SCMs. Let
\mathcal{I}_{L}
and
\mathcal{I}_{H}
be the sets of allowed interventions respectively. Given
\tau:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{H}}
, we say that
(\mathcal{M}_{H},\mathcal{I}_{H})
is a
\tau
-abstraction of
(\mathcal{M}_{L},\mathcal{I}_{L})
if:

1.

\tau
is surjective;

2.

There exists surjective
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{H}}
that is compatible with
\tau
, i.e.

\tau(\mathcal{M}_{L[\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}_{L}))=\mathcal{M}_{H[\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}_{L})),

(59)

for all
\mathbf{u}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}
and all
(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
;

3.

\mathcal{I}_{H}=\omega_{\tau}(\mathcal{I}_{L})
.

\blacksquare

Further, we will assume that if
(\mathcal{M}_{H},\mathcal{I}_{H})
is a
\tau
-abstraction of
(\mathcal{M}_{L},\mathcal{I}_{L})
, then
P(\mathbf{U}_{H})=\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(P(\mathbf{U}_{L}))=P(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{U}_{L}))
, that is, the distribution of
P(\mathbf{U}_{H})
can be obtained from
P(\mathbf{U}_{L})
via the push-forward measure through
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}
. While it is not explicitly stated in the definition, this property aligns with the intention of linking the spaces of
\mathbf{U}_{L}
and
\mathbf{U}_{H}
through
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}
.

16
Strong
\tau
-Abstraction
(
Beckers and Halpern 2019
, Def. 3.15)
.

We say that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is a strong
\tau
-abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
if
(\mathcal{M}_{H},\mathcal{I}_{H})
is a
\tau
-abstraction of
(\mathcal{M}_{L},\mathcal{I}_{L})
and
\mathcal{I}_{H}=\mathcal{I}_{H}^{*}
.

\blacksquare

17
Constructive
\tau
-Abstraction
(
Beckers and Halpern 2019
, Def. 3.19)
.

\mathcal{M}_{H}
is a constructive
\tau
-abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
if
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is a strong
\tau
-abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
, and there exists a partition of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
,
\mathbb{C}=\{\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\}
(where
n=|\mathbf{V}_{H}|
) with nonempty
\mathbf{C}_{1}
to
\mathbf{C}_{n}
, such that
\tau
can be decomposed as
\tau=(\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{1}},\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{2}},\dots,\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{n}})
, where each
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{V_{H,i}}
maps the
i
th partition to the
i
th variable of
\mathbf{V}_{H}
.

\blacksquare

In typical causal inference settings where nature is modeled by an SCM, every possible intervention is well-defined. In practice, some interventions may not intuitively correspond to an explicit action. For example, in a medical dataset, perhaps cholesterol level is measured from each person in the dataset. It may not make sense to consider interventions on cholesterol level, since it is not clear how, in practice, one would fix or change someone’s cholesterol level to specific values. Nonetheless, it is still possible to theoretically study the effects of such an intervention through the semantics of SCMs. Therefore, the notion of “allowed interventions” is not typically discussed outside of works of abstractions as every intervention is well-defined.

This no longer holds true in the discussion of abstractions, which is why definitions of abstractions like Def.
15
explicitly require the allowed interventions to be specified. Notably, since every intervention in an SCM involves the creation of a submodel through the mutilation procedure, an intervention on a lower level SCM may not have an obvious counterpart in the higher level SCM. For an in depth understanding of the intricacies of this, we defer readers to read
Beckers and Halpern 2019
. In this work, given that one would like to use the higher level SCM
\mathcal{M}_{H}
for downstream inference tasks, we provide the maximum possible flexibility and assume that
\mathcal{I}_{H}=\mathcal{I}_{H}^{*}
.

Defining
\mathcal{I}_{L}
is somewhat trickier, since in any nontrivial abstraction, there are cases where an intervention on the lower level does not have an equivalent intervention on the higher level. To decide on a criteria of which interventions are allowed on the lower level model, consider the following lemmas.

3
.

Let
\tau
be a constructive abstraction function w.r.t.
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
. If
\mathbf{X}_{L}
is a union of clusters in
\mathbb{C}
(that is, there exists
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathbf{X}_{L}=\bigcup_{\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}\mathbf{C}_{i}
), then
\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})
exists and is equal to
\tau(\mathbf{X}_{L})\leftarrow\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})
.

\blacksquare

Proof.

If
\mathbf{X}_{L}
is a union of clusters in
\mathbb{C}
, then without loss of generality, suppose it can be decomposed as
\mathbf{X}_{L}=(\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{C}_{k})
, and
\mathbf{V}_{L}\setminus\mathbf{X}_{L}=(\mathbf{C}_{k+1},\mathbf{C}_{k+2},\dots,\mathbf{C}_{n})
. By Def.
6
, we can then compute, for any
\mathbf{v}_{L}\in\rst(\mathbf{V}_{L},\mathbf{x}_{L})
,

\displaystyle\tau(\mathbf{v}_{L})

\displaystyle=\tau((\mathbf{x}_{L},\mathbf{v}_{L}\setminus\mathbf{x}_{L}))

\displaystyle=\tau((\mathbf{c}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{k},\mathbf{c}_{k+1},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n}))

\displaystyle=(\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{1}}(\mathbf{c}_{1}),\dots,\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{k}}(\mathbf{c}_{k}),\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{k+1}}(\mathbf{c}_{k+1}),\dots,\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{n}}(\mathbf{c}_{n}))

\displaystyle=(\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L}),\tau(\mathbf{v}_{L}\setminus\mathbf{x}_{L}))\in\rst(\tau(\mathbf{V}_{L}),\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})).

Moreover, for any
\mathbf{v}_{H}\in\rst(\tau(\mathbf{V}_{L}),\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L}))
, there exists
\mathbf{v}_{L}\in\rst(\mathbf{V}_{L},\mathbf{x}_{L})
such that
\tau(\mathbf{v}_{L})=\mathbf{v}_{H}
. Specifically, if
\mathbf{v}_{H}=(\mathcal{D}^{j_{1}}_{\mathbf{C}_{1}},\mathcal{D}^{j_{2}}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}\dots,\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{n}}^{j_{n}})
, then any
\mathbf{v}_{L}=(\mathbf{c}_{1},\mathbf{c}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n})
satisfies this relationship if
\mathbf{c}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{j_{i}}
for all
i
. Hence, by definition,
\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})
exists and is equal to
\tau(\mathbf{X}_{L})\leftarrow\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})
.
∎

4
.

Let
\tau
be a constructive abstraction function over
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
. For any intervention
\mathbf{X}_{H}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{H}\in\mathcal{I}_{H}^{*}
, there exists
\mathbf{X}_{L}
such that
\mathbf{X}_{L}
is a union of clusters of
\mathbb{C}
, and
\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})=\mathbf{X}_{H}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{H}
.

\blacksquare

Proof.

Without loss of generality, suppose that
\mathbf{X}_{H}=\{X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{k}\}
, corresponding to clusters
\{\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{C}_{k}\}
respectively. Then, choose
\mathbf{X}_{L}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{C}_{i}
, a union of clusters. The proof holds if for each
\mathbf{C}_{i}
, there exist values
\mathbf{c}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
such that
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\mathbf{c}_{i})=x_{i}
. Note that by definition,
x_{i}
corresponds to some
\mathcal{D}^{j}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
such that
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\mathbf{c}_{i})=x_{i}
for all
\mathbf{c}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}^{j}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
. As
\mathcal{D}^{j}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
is an element of a partition of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
, it must be nonempty. Hence, the claim holds, and
\mathbf{x}_{L}
can be constructed by taking the one such value
\mathbf{c}_{i}
for each
i
. Lemma
3
can then be used to show that
\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})=\tau(\mathbf{X}_{L})\leftarrow\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})
, concluding the proof.
∎

In other words, by Lemma
3
, an intervention on a union of intervariable clusters will always have an intuitive well-defined corresponding intervention. Moreover, Lemma
4
shows that all high level interventions are accounted for on the lower level model
16
16

16

There are some contrived settings in which
\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})
still exists even when
\mathbf{X}_{L}
is not a union of clusters, but it is inconsequential to omit these cases since all possible high level interventions are covered by Lemma
4
.
. Hence, we make the assumption that
\mathcal{I}_{L}
is defined such that
\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L}\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
if and only if
\mathbf{X}_{L}
is a union of clusters of
\mathbb{C}
. If, for some reason, this choice of
\mathcal{I}_{L}
is not desirable for some application, it may indicate that the choice of clusters,
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
, should be revised. Under these assumptions, the work in the main text can be presented without the need to explicitly consider allowed interventions, simplifying the discussion.

We now show the main connection between this work and established works by proving Prop.
1
.

See
1

Proof.

For this proof, define
\mathcal{M}_{L}=\langle\mathbf{U}_{L},\mathbf{V}_{L},\mathcal{F}_{L},P(\mathbf{U}_{L})\rangle
and
\mathcal{M}_{H}=\langle\mathbf{U}_{H},\mathbf{V}_{H},\mathcal{F}_{H},P(\mathbf{U}_{H})\rangle
.

We first show the forward direction: if
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistent with
\mathcal{M}_{L}
, then there exists SCM
\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{H})
and
\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\prime}
is a constructive
\tau
-abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
.

We will start this proof by first attempting to show that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
itself is a constructive
\tau
-abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
. We first note that the intervariable clustering
\mathbb{C}
is defined to be a partition of a subset of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
, and
\tau
is constructed by Def.
6
, which is specifically defined to be decomposed into subfunctions
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
mapping cluster
\mathbf{C}_{i}
to a corresponding
V_{H,i}
for each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. If we further define
\mathbf{C}_{n+1}=\mathbf{V}_{L}\setminus\bigcup_{\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbf{C}_{i}
(variables that are projected out through the abstraction), then we see that
\{\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{C}_{n+1}\}
forms a partition over
\mathbf{V}_{L}
.

What remains is to show that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is a strong
\tau
-abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
. As
\mathcal{I}_{H}=\mathcal{I}_{H}^{*}
by assumption, this reduces to showing that it is a
\tau
-abstraction. We show that the three requirements of Def.
15
hold:

1.

Consider any
\mathbf{v}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{H}}
. For all
V_{H,i}\in\mathbf{V}_{H}
, note that
V_{H,i}
must correspond to some
\mathbf{C}_{i}
, and
v_{H,i}
must correspond to some
\mathcal{D}^{j}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
by definition. Since
\mathcal{D}^{j}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
is part of a partition, it must be nonempty, so
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\mathbf{c}_{i})=v_{H,i}
for any choice of
\mathbf{c}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}^{j}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
. Hence,
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
is surjective for all
i
, implying that
\tau
is surjective as it is simply a collection of all
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
.

2.

Consider the functional counterfactual set
\mathbf{F}_{H}
of
\mathcal{M}_{H}
(as defined in Eq.
57
). For every
\mathbf{f}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{F}_{H}}
, consider the counterfactual quantity

\begin{split}&P(\mathbf{F}_{H}=\mathbf{f}_{H})=\\
&P\left(\bigwedge_{V_{H,i}\in\mathbf{V}_{H}}\bigwedge_{\mathbf{pa}_{V_{H,i}}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{H,i}}}}V_{H,i\left[\mathbf{pa}_{V_{H_{i}}}^{(j)}\right]}=v_{H,i}^{(j)}\right).\end{split}

By Eq.
10
, this is equal to

\begin{split}&P(\mathbf{F}_{H}=\mathbf{f}_{H})=\\
&\sum_{\forall i,j\mathbf{c}_{L,i}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}:\tau(\mathbf{c}_{L,i}^{(j)})=v_{H,i}^{(j)}}P\left(\bigwedge_{i,j}\mathbf{C}_{L,i\left[\mathbf{x}_{L,i}^{(j)}\right]}=\mathbf{c}_{L,i}^{(j)}\right)\end{split}

for all choices of
\mathbf{x}_{L,i}^{(j)}
such that
\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L,i}^{(j)})=\mathbf{pa}_{V_{H,i}}^{(j)}
. Recall that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{H}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{H}}
is the set of values of
\mathbf{U}_{H}
such that
\mathbf{u}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{H}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})
if and only if
\mathbf{F}_{H}=\mathbf{f}_{H}
when
\mathbf{U}_{H}=\mathbf{u}_{H}
. Similarly, define
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}
as the set of values of
\mathbf{U}_{L}
such that
\mathbf{u}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})
if and only if
\bigwedge_{i,j}\mathbf{C}_{L,i\left[\mathbf{x}_{L,i}^{(j)}\right]}=\mathbf{c}_{L,i}^{(j)}
when
\mathbf{U}_{L}=\mathbf{u}_{L}
.

Note that there may exist
\mathbf{u}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}
that do not belong to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})
for any choice of
\mathbf{f}_{H}
. However, the total probability measure of all such cases must be 0, since
P(\mathbf{U}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}(\mathbf{f}_{H}))=P(\mathbf{U}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{H}}(\mathbf{f}_{H}))
, and
\{\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{H}}(\mathbf{f}_{H}):\mathbf{f}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{F}_{H}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})\neq\emptyset\}
forms a partition over
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{H}}
by Lemma
1
and must therefore have probability 1. Hence, we can define
\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}
with domain
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}}
that is equivalent to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}
but with these cases omitted. Correspondingly, we can define
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
as
\mathcal{F}
but excluding the outputs when
\mathbf{U}_{L}
takes a value not in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}}
. We can then define
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}=\langle\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L},\mathbf{V}_{L},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L},P(\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L})\rangle
, where
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{L})
because only a measure zero portion of
P(\mathbf{U}_{L})
was removed.

Define
U^{\prime}
as a random variable with domain
\mathcal{D}_{U^{\prime}}=\{u^{\prime}_{\mathbf{f}_{H}}:\mathbf{f}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{F}_{H}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})=\emptyset\}
, that is, a value for each choice of
\mathbf{f}_{H}
with a nonempty
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})
. Define
P(U^{\prime}=u^{\prime}_{\mathbf{f}_{H}})=P(\mathbf{F}_{H}=\mathbf{f}_{H})
. Choose
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}}
such that
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})=u^{\prime}_{\mathbf{f}_{H}}
if and only if
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})
. Note that this function is surjective because
u^{\prime}_{\mathbf{f}_{H}}\in\mathcal{D}_{U^{\prime}}
only if
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})
is nonempty. Furthermore,
P(U^{\prime}=u^{\prime}_{\mathbf{f}_{H}})=P(\mathbf{F}_{H}=\mathbf{f}_{H})=P(\mathbf{u}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}(\mathbf{f}_{H}))
, so the probability distributions are consistent.

It is not necessarily the case that property 2 of
\tau
-abstractions holds between
\mathcal{M}_{H}
and
\mathcal{M}_{L}
, but we can create a new SCM
\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\prime}=\langle\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{H}=\{U^{\prime}\},\mathbf{V}_{H},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{H},P(\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{H})\rangle
such that the property holds between
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
. Define
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{H}=\{f^{\prime H}_{V_{H,i}}:V_{H,i}\in\mathbf{V}_{H}\}
such that each
f^{\prime H}_{V_{H,i}}(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{H,i}},u^{\prime}_{\mathbf{f}_{H}})=f^{H}_{V_{H,i}}(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{H,i}},\mathbf{u}_{H})
if and only if
\mathbf{u}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{H}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})
. Given that
P(U^{\prime}=u^{\prime}_{\mathbf{f}_{H}})=P(\mathbf{F}_{H}=\mathbf{f}_{H})=P(\mathbf{u}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{H}}(\mathbf{f}_{H}))
, this implies that
P^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H}}(\mathbf{F}_{H}=\mathbf{f}_{H})=P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(\mathbf{F}_{H}=\mathbf{f}_{H})
, further implying that
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{H})
by Lemma
2
.

We now show that
\tau(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L[\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H[\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
, for all
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}}
and all
(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
. For the rest of the proof, assume that any notation involving subscripts
L
and
H
refers to
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H}
rather than
\mathcal{M}_{L}
and
\mathcal{M}_{H}
. By Lemma
3
, we know that
\omega(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})=\tau(\mathbf{X}_{L})\leftarrow\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})
, so let
\mathbf{x}_{H}=\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})
. Assume on the contrary that there exists
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}}
and
(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
such that this claim does not hold. Then there must exist
\mathbf{C}_{L,i}
(and
V_{H,i}=\tau(\mathbf{C}_{L,i})
) such that
\tau(\mathbf{C}_{L,i[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))\neq V_{H,i[\mathbf{x}_{H}]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})))
. Let
\mathbf{f}_{H}
be the value of
\mathbf{F}_{H}
such that this value of
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}(\mathbf{f}_{H})
. We show using proof by induction that this poses a contradiction.

There must be a topological ordering to
\mathbf{V}_{H}
as
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H}
is recursive. In the base case, assume that
\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{H,i}}=\emptyset
. This means that
\tau(\mathbf{C}_{L,i[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\tau(\mathbf{C}_{L,i[\emptyset]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
and
V_{H,i[\mathbf{x}_{H}]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})))=V_{H,i[\emptyset]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))))
. Note that
\tau(\mathbf{C}_{L,i[\emptyset]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\tau(\mathbf{c}_{L,i}^{(j)})=v_{H,i}^{(j)}=V_{H,i[\emptyset]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
by definition of
\mathbf{f}_{H}
, contradicting the claim that
\tau(\mathbf{C}_{L,i[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))\neq V_{H,i[\mathbf{x}_{H}]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})))
.

Now assume for the sake of induction that
\tau(\mathbf{C}_{L,i^{\prime}[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=V_{H,i^{\prime}[\mathbf{x}_{H}]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})))
for all
V_{H,i^{\prime}}\in\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{H,i}}
. Note that
\tau(\mathbf{C}_{L,i[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\tau(\mathbf{C}_{L,i[\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{L,i}[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
and
V_{H,i[\mathbf{x}_{H}]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})))=V_{H,i[\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{H,i}[\mathbf{x}_{H}]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))))
due to Corol.
3
. However,
\tau(\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{L,i}[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{H,i}[\mathbf{x}_{H}]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
by the inductive hypothesis. This means that
\tau(\mathbf{C}_{L,i[\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{L,i}[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\tau(\mathbf{c}_{L,i}^{(j)})=v_{H,i}^{(j)}=V_{H,i[\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{H,i}[\mathbf{x}_{H}]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))))
from
\mathbf{f}_{H}
, once again contradicting the claim that
\tau(\mathbf{C}_{L,i[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))\neq V_{H,i[\mathbf{x}_{H}]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})))
.

Therefore, it must be the case that
\tau(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L[\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H[\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
, for all
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}}
and all
(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
.

3.

\mathcal{I}_{H}=\omega_{\tau}(\mathcal{I}_{L})
holds by the assumption of
\mathcal{I}_{L}
and Lemma
4
.

This completes the forward direction of the proof.

We now show the backward direction: if there exists SCMs
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
and
\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{L})
,
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{H})
, and
\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\prime}
is a constructive
\tau
-abstraction of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
, then
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistent with
\mathcal{M}_{L}
. It is sufficient to simply show that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H}
is
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistent with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
, since if
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{L})
and
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{H})
, this would also imply that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistent with
\mathcal{M}_{L}
.

This can be proven by showing that Eq.
10
holds, that is

\begin{split}&\sum_{\forall i\mathbf{y}_{L,i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Y}_{L,i}}:\tau(\mathbf{y}_{L,i})=\mathbf{y}_{H,i}}\hskip-42.67912ptP^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}}(\mathbf{y}_{L,1[\mathbf{x}_{L,1}]},\mathbf{y}_{L,2[\mathbf{x}_{L,2}]},\dots)\\
&=P^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H}}(\mathbf{y}_{H,1[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{H,1})]},\mathbf{y}_{H,2[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L,2})]},\dots)\end{split}

for all choices of
\mathbf{y}_{H,i}
and
\mathbf{x}_{H,i}
. Denote
Q
as the l.h.s. of the equation and
\tau(Q)
as the r.h.s. Denote
\mathcal{D}_{Q}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}}
as the set of values of
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}
such that
\bigwedge_{i}\mathbf{Y}_{L,i[\mathbf{x}_{L,i}]}=\mathbf{y}_{L,i[\mathbf{x}_{L,i}]}
for all
\mathbf{y}_{L,i}
such that
\tau(\mathbf{y}_{L,i})=\mathbf{y}_{H,i}
. Similarly, denote
\mathcal{D}_{\tau(Q)}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{H}}
as the set of values of
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{H}
such that
\bigwedge_{i}\mathbf{Y}_{H,i[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L,i})]}=\mathbf{y}_{H,i[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L,i})]}
. Note that
Q=P(\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{Q})
and
\tau(Q)=P(\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\tau(Q)})
. We claim that
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{Q}
if and only if
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})\in\mathcal{D}_{\tau(Q)}
.

By definition of constructive
\tau
-abstractions, there must exist
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}
such that
\tau(\mathcal{M}_{L[\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\mathcal{M}_{H[\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
, for all
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}}
and all
(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
, and further that
P(\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{H})=P(\tau(\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}))
by assumption. This implies that
\tau(\mathbf{Y}_{L,i[\mathbf{x}_{L,i}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\mathbf{Y}_{H,i[\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{x}_{L,i})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\mathbf{Y}_{H,i[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
for all
i
by Lemma
3
. Hence, if
\mathbf{Y}_{L,i[\mathbf{x}_{i}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})=\mathbf{y}_{L,i}
, where
\tau(\mathbf{y}_{L,i})=\mathbf{y}_{H,i}
, then
\mathbf{Y}_{H,i[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\mathbf{y}_{H,i}
. Considering this for all values of
i
, it must be the case that
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{Q}
if and only if
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})\in\mathcal{D}_{\tau(Q)}
.

Since
P(\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{H})=P(\tau(\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}))
, this implies that
\tau(Q)=P(\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\tau(Q)})=P(\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{Q})=Q
, concluding the proof.
∎

We note that the theorem does not claim
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistency is equivalent to constructive
\tau
-abstractions, rather making a weaker claim that there must exist a pair of (potentially different) SCMs that are
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-equivalent and fit the definition of a constructive
\tau
-abstraction. The reason is that, in fact, the definition for constructive
\tau
-abstraction is stricter than
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistency, but as evident in the proof, the only restriction is on the domains of
\mathbf{U}_{L}
and
\mathbf{U}_{H}
. In the case of
\mathbf{U}_{L}
, there may be a measure zero portion of the domain
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}
that do not translate to the higher level functional counterfactuals, and in the case of
\mathbf{U}_{H}
, it is possible that the space of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{H}}
may not allow for
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}
to be surjective. However, the theorem still essentially states that they are equivalent, at least on the three levels of the PCH. We therefore argue that, given the unobserved nature of the exogenous variables and the generating SCM, these two concepts are equivalent on a practical level.

We now prove that Alg.
1
successfully returns an
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistent model.

See
3

Proof.

We can show this result by first showing that the output of Alg.
1
,
\mathcal{M}_{H}
, is a constructive
\tau
-abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
. By Def.
6
, it is clear by construction that
\tau
can be decomposed as
\tau=(\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{1}},\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{2}},\dots,\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{n}})
, and each
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
maps the
i
th partition to the
i
th variable of
\mathbf{V}_{H}
, as established on line 2. Hence, we must simply show that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is a
\tau
-abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
. Out of the three properties of Def.
15
, properties 1 and 3 can be proven similarly to how it is done in the forward direction of Prop.
1
. Then, to prove property 2, we must simply show that there exists surjective
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{H}}
such that
P(\mathbf{U}_{H})=\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(P(\mathbf{U}_{L}))=P(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{U}_{L}))
, and
\tau(\mathcal{M}_{L[\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}_{L}))=\mathcal{M}_{H[\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}_{L}))
, for all
\mathbf{u}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}
and all
(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
.

The choice of
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}
is simple—we can use the identity function as by construction in line 1,
\mathbf{U}_{H}=\mathbf{U}_{L}
and
P(\mathbf{U}_{H})=P(\mathbf{U}_{L})
. Further, note that
\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})=\tau(\mathbf{X}_{L})\leftarrow\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})
by Lemma
3
. Hence, we must simply show that
\tau(\mathcal{M}_{L[\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}_{L}))=\mathcal{M}_{H[\tau(\mathbf{X}_{L})\leftarrow\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})
.

We start by showing that
V_{i[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})=\tau(\mathbf{C}_{i[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}_{L}))
for every
V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}_{H}
and corresponding
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
. Note that for every
V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}_{H}
, line 5 dictates that

V_{i}\leftarrow f_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}},\mathbf{u}_{V_{i}})=\tau\left(f_{V}^{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{pa}}_{V},\mathbf{u}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{i}\right),

which implies that

V_{i[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})=\tau\left(f_{V}^{L}\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{pa}}_{V},\mathbf{u}_{V}\right):V\in\mathbf{C}_{i}\right),

(60)

where
\mathbf{u}_{V}
is compatible with
\mathbf{u}_{L}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{pa}}_{V}
refers to any value
\mathbf{pa}_{V}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Pa}_{V}}
such that
\tau(\mathbf{pa}_{V})=\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})
. Note that since
\mathcal{M}_{L}
satisfies the abstract invariance condition (AIC) w.r.t.
\tau
, the value of
f^{L}_{V}
will not change based on this choice of
\mathbf{pa}_{V}
.

We continue using proof by induction. Since
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is recursive, this implies there is a topological ordering of the functions of
\mathcal{M}_{H}
. In the base case, if
\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}=\emptyset
, then

V_{i[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})=V_{i[\emptyset]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})=\tau\left(f_{V}^{L}(\mathbf{u}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{i}\right)=\tau(\mathbf{C}_{i}(\mathbf{u}_{L})),

which aligns with the claim. For the inductive hypothesis, assume that
V_{j[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})=\tau(\mathbf{C}_{j[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}_{L}))
for every
V_{j}\in\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}
. Then we have

\displaystyle V_{i[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})

\displaystyle=V_{i[\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})

by Corol.
3

\displaystyle=V_{i[\tau(\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}_{L}))]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})

by inductive hypothesis

\displaystyle=\tau\left(f_{V}^{L}(\mathbf{Pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}_{L}),\mathbf{u}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{i}\right)

by Eq.
60

\displaystyle=\tau(\mathbf{C}_{i[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}_{L}))

simplification,

proving the claim. Since
V_{i[\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\mathbf{u}_{L})=\tau(\mathbf{C}_{i[\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}_{L}))
for every
V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}_{H}
, this implies that
\tau(\mathcal{M}_{L[\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}_{L}))=\mathcal{M}_{H[\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}_{L}))
, proving that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is a constructive
\tau
-abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
. Finally, Prop.
1
proves that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
must therefore be an
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
.
∎

We leverage this property to prove that the abstract invariance condition (AIC) is necessary and sufficient for the existence of an abstraction.

See
2

Proof.

If
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
satisfies the AIC w.r.t.
\tau
, then one can use Alg.
1
to obtain an example of a model that is
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistent with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
(and therefore with
\mathcal{M}_{L}
), as proven in Prop.
3
.

We now consider the other direction. If
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistent with
\mathcal{M}_{L}
, then by Prop.
1
, there must exist some
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H}
such that
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{L})
,
\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H})=\mathcal{L}_{3}(\mathcal{M}_{H})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H}
is a constructive
\tau
-abstraction of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
. For the rest of this proof, assume that any terms with a subscripts of
L
or
H
refer to
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
or
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H}
instead of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
and
\mathcal{M}_{H}
. The constructive
\tau
-abstraction property implies that there exists surjective
\tau_{\mathbf{U}}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{H}}
such that
\tau(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L[\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H[\omega_{\tau}(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
, for all
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}}
and all
(\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L})\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
. By Lemma
3
, this means
\tau(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L[\mathbf{X}_{L}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{L}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H[\tau(\mathbf{X}_{L})\leftarrow\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that it is not the case that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
satisfies the AIC w.r.t.
\tau
. Then there must exist
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}
such that
\tau(\mathbf{v}_{1})=\tau(\mathbf{v}_{2})
, yet

\begin{split}&\tau\left(\left(f^{L}_{V}(\mathbf{pa}_{V}^{(1)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{i}\right)\right)\\
&\neq\tau\left(\left(f^{L}_{V}(\mathbf{pa}_{V}^{(2)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{i}\right)\right)\end{split}

for some value of
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{L}}
and
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.

This would imply that
\tau\left(\mathbf{C}_{i[\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(1)}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})\right)\neq\tau\left(\mathbf{C}_{i[\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(2)}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})\right)
, so
\tau\left(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L[\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(1)}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})\right)\neq\tau\left(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L[\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(2)}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})\right)
. By the
\tau
-abstraction definition, we have
\tau\left(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L[\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(1)}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})\right)=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H[\tau(\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(1)})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
and
\tau\left(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L[\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(2)}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})\right)=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{H[\tau(\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(2)})]}(\tau_{\mathbf{U}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L}))
. However, since
\tau(\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(1)})=\tau(\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(2)})
, this implies that
\tau\left(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L[\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(1)}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})\right)=\tau\left(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L[\mathbf{pa}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}^{(2)}]}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{L})\right)
, contradicting the earlier statement. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
must satisfy the AIC w.r.t.
\tau
, completing the proof.
∎

A.3
Proofs of Sec.
3

We start by noting the impossibility of performing causal inferences without additional assumptions, as implied by the Causal Hierarchy Theorem.

3
Causal Hierarchy Theorem (CHT)
(
Bareinboim et al. 2022
, Thm. 1)
.

Let
\Omega^{*}
be the set of all SCMs. We say that Layer
j
of the causal hierarchy for SCMs
collapses
to Layer
i
(
i<j
) relative to
\mathcal{M}^{*}\in\Omega^{*}
if
L_{i}(\mathcal{M}^{*})=L_{i}(\mathcal{M})
implies that
L_{j}(\mathcal{M}^{*})=L_{j}(\mathcal{M})
for all
\mathcal{M}\in\Omega^{*}
. Then, with respect to the Lebesgue measure over (a suitable encoding of
L_{3}
-equivalence classes of) SCMs, the subset in which Layer
j
of SCMs collapses to Layer
i
is measure zero.

\blacksquare

Given this result, we note that the same principle applies to performing causal inferences across abstractions.

5
.

Let
\Omega_{L}
and
\Omega_{H}
be the space of SCMs defined over
\mathbf{V}_{L}
and
\mathbf{V}_{H}
respectively, and let
\tau:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{H}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}
be a constructive abstraction function defined over clusters
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
. Let
\Omega^{\prime}_{L}
be the subset of
\Omega_{L}
that satisfies the AIC. Define
\psi_{\tau}:\Omega^{\prime}_{L}\rightarrow\Omega_{H}
such that
\psi_{\tau}(\mathcal{M}_{L})=\mathcal{M}_{H}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{H}\in\Omega_{H}
is
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistent with
\mathcal{M}_{L}
(while there could be many such SCMs, they are all
\mathcal{L}_{3}
