The term “non-causal” is very broad and could encompass many different types of relationships. Studying specific types of non-causal relationships is out of the scope of this work, so we will treat these relationships in the same way. For every subset of variables (say
\mathbf{Z}\subseteq\mathbf{V}_{L}
) related through some non-causal relationship, we will assume that there is some function
f_{\mathbf{Z}}
that takes as input variables from
\mathbf{U}_{L}
and
\mathbf{V}_{L}
and maps it jointly to the space of
\mathbf{Z}
. We note that this is a general encoding of such relationships that makes minimal assumptions. For example, perhaps the relationship between three variables,
X
,
Z
, and
Y
can be described using a Markov random field like shown in Fig.
17
, where
X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}Y\mid Z
. Our assumption states that the behavior of these three variables can be abstracted into one function
f_{\{X,Z,Y\}}
which outputs values of
X
,
Z
, and
Y
, therefore losing the independence information. (It is possible, however, that these could be implicitly encoded through the intravariable clusters). These non-causal relationships can be expressed graphically.

21
Non-causal Graph.

Let
\mathbf{V}_{L}
be a set of variables and
\overline{\mathcal{G}}=\langle\mathbf{V}_{L},\mathbf{E}\rangle
be an undirected graph with nodes representing
\mathbf{V}_{L}
. Then, an edge
(V_{1},V_{2})
is in
\mathbf{E}
if and only if there is some non-causal relationship between
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
.

\blacksquare

We will assume that, in settings which intervariable clusters must be learned, we are given knowledge of the existence of non-causal relationships through a non-causal graph of the lowest level variables
\mathbf{V}_{L}
. This information could be acquired simply as an assumption of the user, as they are typically only present at the lowest levels of abstraction (e.g. the relationship between pixels in an image or between atoms of an object). Under this assumption, distributions over
\mathbf{V}_{L}
can be treated as if variables in the same connected component in
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
share a common cause, and they can be factorized as such.

Condition C2.

By definition of admissibility, two variables should not be in the same intervariable cluster if doing so forms a cycle in the order of the functions or graph. This condition can be verified given causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
of the low level variables
\mathbf{V}_{L}
.

Condition C3.

C3 and C4 depend on the user’s needs based on the queries of interest,
\mathbb{Q}
, in downstream tasks. The ideal level of abstraction can be determined by the groupings of variables in these queries. C3 essentially enforces that the level of abstraction should be kept low enough to retain the ability to differentiate the nuances between individual variables of interest in the queries. For example, if one would like to study the causal effect of a drug
X
on recovery rate
Y
, but
Y
was clustered with another variable
Z
representing blood pressure, then it no longer becomes possible to answer queries specifically about
Y
without including
Z
. This is formally defined below.

22
Cluster Answerability.

A counterfactual query
Q=P^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(\mathbf{Y}_{1[\mathbf{x}_{1}]},\mathbf{Y}_{2[\mathbf{x}_{2}]},\dots)
is answerable from intervariable clusters
\mathbb{C}
if and only if for all
\mathbf{Y}_{i}
(and
\mathbf{X}_{i}
), there exists
\mathbb{C}_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathbf{Y}_{i}=\bigcup_{\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{C}_{i}}\mathbf{C}
.

\blacksquare

In other words, all queries should be written in terms of unions of clusters.

Condition C4.

Even if answerability is not violated, clustering variables together or projecting them out will always result in a loss of information. Such a loss may affect the identifiability of the queries. Ideally, one would not want to drop information that would change the status of an already identifiable query, and this is enforced by C4.

Condition C5.

Finally, C5 is usually desirable following the idea of Occam’s razor, i.e., all else being equal, simplicity should be preferred. We define coarseness as follows.

23
Coarseness.

Let
\mathbb{C}_{1}
and
\mathbb{C}_{2}
be two intervariable clusterings of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
.
\mathbb{C}_{1}
is said to be coarser than
\mathbb{C}_{2}
(equivalently,
\mathbb{C}_{2}
is finer than
\mathbb{C}_{1}
) if and only if for every
\mathbf{C}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}_{2}
, either
\mathbf{C}_{2}\cap\bigcup_{\mathbf{C}_{1}\in\mathbb{C}_{1}}\mathbf{C}_{1}=\emptyset
, or there exists
\mathbf{C}_{1}\in\mathbb{C}_{1}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{2}\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{1}
.

With this definition, the goal is to find a maximally coarse clustering
\mathbb{C}
that satisfies conditions 1-4. That is, there should not exist a coarser clustering
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
that also satisfies the conditions, although the coarsest clustering is not necessarily unique.

It turns out that the set of all possible maximally course clusterings can be “bounded” in a sense using C1, C2, and C3. The “minimally” coarse clustering can be described with the following lemma.

7
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
be a non-causal graph over
\mathbf{V}_{L}
. Let
\mathbb{C}_{\min}
be the intervariable clustering of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
composed of the connected components of
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
. Then, any clustering
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
violates condition C1 if and only if it is not equal to or coarser than
\mathbb{C}_{\min}
.

\blacksquare

Proof.

We first note that
\mathbb{C}_{\min}
does not violate condition C1 because by construction, if there exists a noncausal edge between
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
in
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
, then they must be in the same cluster. Any coarser clustering has the same property.

If
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
is not equal to or coarser than
\mathbb{C}_{\min}
, that means that there exists
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{C}_{\min}
such that
\mathbf{C}\not\subseteq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
for all
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
. This implies that there exists
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathbf{C}
such that belong to different clusters
\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{C}_{2}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
. However, since
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
were in the same cluster in
\mathbb{C}_{\min}
, there must exist some non-causal path from
V_{1}
to
V_{2}
in
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
. This means that there is a noncausal connection between clusters
\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{C}_{2}^{\prime}
, so
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
violates condition C1.
∎

Intuitively, the minimally coarse clustering must at least cover the connected components of
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
to abstract away the non-causal relations and satisfy condition C1. Additionally, admissibility (C2) adds another constraint.

8
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a set of intervariable clusters that are not admissible w.r.t.
\mathcal{G}
. Let
\mathbb{C}^{*}
be the set of clusters that is coarser than
\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{1}
and
\mathbf{C}_{2}
in
\mathbb{C}
are merged in
\mathbb{C}^{*}
if and only if
\mathbf{C}_{1}
and
\mathbf{C}_{2}
are in a cycle in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, any set of clusters that are coarser than
\mathbb{C}
and are admissible w.r.t.
\mathcal{G}
are equal to or coarser than
\mathbb{C}^{*}

\blacksquare

Proof.

First, we note that
\mathbb{C}^{*}
is admissible w.r.t.
\mathcal{G}
by construction, since all cycles have been merged. If
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
is a clustering that is coarser than
\mathbb{C}
but not
\mathbb{C}^{*}
, then there must exist
\mathbf{C}^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that
\mathbf{C}^{*}\subsetneq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
for all
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
. Then, there must exist
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathbf{C}^{*}
such that
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are in different clusters in
\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
. However, this implies that there is still a cycle between
\mathbf{C}_{1}
and
\mathbf{C}_{2}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
, breaking admissibility.
∎

. Blue outline illustrates changes in clustering between each graph.

On the other hand, the maximally coarse clustering is related to the concept of maximally answerable clusters, defined below.

24
Maximally Answerable Clusters.

Let
\mathbb{Q}
be a set of counterfactual queries over variables
\mathbf{V}_{L}
, and let
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}
be the set of all subsets of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
that are used in a term in
\mathbb{Q}
(i.e. for every counterfactual term
\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{x}}
in any of the queries, both
\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{X}
should be included in
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}
). Denote
\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}
as the extension of
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}
closed under intersection, that is,
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}\subseteq\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}\Rightarrow V_{1}\cap V_{2}\in\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Then, an intervariable clustering
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
is called the maximally answerable cluster w.r.t.
\mathbb{Q}
if and only if
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
is the set of atoms of
\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, or in other words,
\mathbf{V}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}_{\max}
if and only if
\mathbf{V}_{i}\in\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and there exists no
\mathbf{V}_{j}\in\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
\mathbf{V}_{j}\subsetneq\mathbf{V}_{i}
.

\blacksquare

The concept of maximally answerable clusters can be best shown through an example.

15
.

Suppose

\mathbf{V}_{L}=\{A,B,C,D,E,F,G,H,I,X,Z\}

and the queries of interest
\mathbb{Q}=\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}
are as follows:

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle=P(\{a,b,c,d,e\}_{x})

\displaystyle Q_{2}

\displaystyle=P(\{c,d,e,f,g,h\}_{x,z})

\displaystyle Q_{3}

\displaystyle=P(\{e,f,h,i,z\}_{x}).

We first dissect the terms in the queries, including the subscripts, to obtain
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Doing so yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}

\displaystyle=\{\{A,B,C,D,E\},\{C,D,E,F,G,H\},

\displaystyle\{E,F,H,I,Z\},\{X\},\{X,Z\}\}.

These sets are shown at the top of Fig.
19
. Note that many of the sets overlap. The set of nonoverlapping subregions becomes the maximally answerable clusters, as shown at the bottom of Fig.
19
. In this case, this means

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{\max}

\displaystyle=\{\{A,B\},\{C,D\},\{E\},\{F,H\},

\displaystyle\{G\},\{I\},\{X\},\{Z\}\}.

Note that no set in
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
overlaps with each other, but all sets are subsets of some set in
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

\blacksquare

It turns out that the set of maximally answerable clusters is also the maximally coarse clustering that does not violate condition C3, as shown below.

9
.

Let
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
be the intervariable clustering of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
that is the maximally answerable cluster with respect to a set of counterfactual queries
\mathbb{Q}
. Then, any clustering
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
violates condition C3 if and only if it is not equal to or finer than
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
.

\blacksquare

Proof.

We first note that
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
does not violate condition C3, since if there existed a query with a term set
\mathbf{Z}
such that
\mathbf{Z}
is not a union of clusters of
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
, then either there exists
Z\in\mathbf{Z}
such that
Z
is not in any set of
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
, or
Z\in\mathbf{C}
for some
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{C}_{\max}
such that
\mathbf{C}\subsetneq\mathbf{Z}
. However, the first case is impossible since if
Z\in\mathbf{Z}
, then
Z
must be added to some set in
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The second case is not possible either because then
\mathbf{Z}\cap\mathbf{C}\neq\emptyset
, which means that the clusters of
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
could be divided further, contradicting its definition. If
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
does not violate condition C3, then no finer clustering can violate it either because a union of clusters in
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
can always translate to a union of clusters in a finer clustering.

If
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
is not equal to or finer than
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
, then there exists at least one cluster
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\subsetneq\mathbf{C}
for all
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{C}_{\max}
. This means that there exist
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
such that
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are not in the same cluster in
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
. This implies that there exists at least one query such that one of its terms contains one of
V_{1}
or
V_{2}
but not the other, in which case, such a term cannot be described as a union of clusters from
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
, violating condition C3.
∎

Figure 19:
Top:
A venn diagram of all of the variables and what set of
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}
to which they belong.
Bottom:
The collection of nonoverlapping intersections of all sets that form the maximum answerable clusters.

Intuitively, we cannot simply cluster all variables together because we may want to answer queries that require separate consideration of different sets of variables. At best, we can only cluster together terms that always appear together in all queries. Additionally, we note that finding the maximally answerable clusters can be done in polynomial time. One simple approach is to add one set of
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}
at a time, repeatedly computing the intersection with the existing clusters. At any point, there can be at most
n=|\mathbf{V}_{L}|
clusters.

With these points in mind, we provide Alg.
3
for learning intervariable clusters. We note that Alg.
3
returns a maximally course clustering satisfying all conditions.

11
.

Alg.
3
returns a maximally course clustering that satisfies C1-4, or returns
FAIL
if one does not exist.

\blacksquare

Proof.

We first note that if
FAIL
is returned, then there does not exist a clustering that satisfies all four conditions. Notably, if
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
is not coarser than
\mathbb{C}_{\min}
at the beginning, then
FAIL
is returned, as a consequence of Lemmas
7
,
8
, and
9
.
FAIL
is also returned if
\mathbb{C}_{\min}
does not satisfy identifiability of all queries, since further clustering will only remove edges and nodes and cannot make a non-ID query turn ID.

If
\mathbb{C}
is returned at the end, then
\mathbb{C}
must satisfy all four conditions. It satisfies conditions C1 and C3 because it will always be coarser than
\mathbb{C}_{\min}
but finer than
\mathbb{C}_{\max}
. It satisfies conditions C2 and C4 because
\mathbb{C}
is never updated unless the “valid” subroutine returns
True
, which implies that C2 and C4 hold.

Finally,
\mathbb{C}
must be maximal. If a clustering violates C4, then there is no coarser clustering that does not violate C4 since clustering only further reduces information. If a clustering does not violate C2, and a coarser clustering exists that also does not violate C2, then there must exist a way to either merge pairs of clusters or remove variables (namely in reverse topological order) such that each intermediate clustering also does not violate C2. Then, the loop ensures that if a variable could be removed, or two clusters could be merged without violating the conditions, then it will be done.
∎

Finally, we note that Alg.
3
runs in polynomial time in terms of
n=|\mathbf{V}_{L}|
and
|\mathbb{Q}|
, as long as checking identification takes polynomial time. Finding
\mathbb{C}_{\min}
from Lem.
7
takes polynomial time because finding connected components in an undirected graph takes polynomial time. Checking admissibility simply requires checking cycles, which can also be done in polynomial time. Finding the maximally answerable clusters can also be done in polynomial time, as discussed earlier.

Algorithm 3

Choosing intervariable clusters.

Consider the following example for intuition on choosing the best set of clusters.

16
.

Consider a setting of annotated image data where

\mathbf{V}_{L}=\{A,T,G,H_{1},H_{2},O_{1},O_{2},O_{3},O_{4},P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4}\},

and the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
(and noncausal diagram
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
) are given in Fig.
18(a)
. The data is collected from people, and the variables are age (
A
), gender (
G
), testosterone level (
T
), mustache hairs (
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
), atoms of other mustache hairs (
O_{1},O_{2},O_{3},O_{4}
), and pixels of an image of the person
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4})
. The queries of interest are
\mathbb{Q}=\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}
, where

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle=P(\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}_{h_{1},h_{2},o_{1},o_{2},o_{3},o_{4}})

\displaystyle Q_{2}

\displaystyle=P(\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}_{h_{1},h_{2},o_{1},o_{2},o_{3},o_{4},g})

\displaystyle Q_{3}

\displaystyle=P(\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}_{a}).

Evidently, the variables depicted in Fig.
18(a)
are too complex, and some intervariable clustering is needed. For starters, there are interactions between the atom variables (
O
) and the pixel variables (
P
) that are too low-level to be considered causally. They should be clustered together, as described in Lemma
7
. This provides the graph in Fig.
18(b)
, in which all of the
O
variables are clustered into another hair variable
H_{3}
, and the pixel variables
P
are clustered into a single image variable
I
. Formally, this clustering is

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\{A\},\{T\},\{G\},\{H_{1}\},

\displaystyle\{H_{2}\},H_{3}=\{O_{1},O_{2},O_{3},O_{4}\},I=\{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4}\}\}.

While this choice of
\mathbb{C}_{1}
is valid in that it satisfies conditions C1-4, there exists a coarser clustering that satisfies the conditions, shown in Fig.
18(c)
. This one is obtained by further clustering
H_{1},H_{2},H_{3}
into a single “mustache” variable
M
. Formally, this clustering is

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{\{A\},\{T\},\{G\},M=\{H_{1},H_{2},O_{1},O_{2},O_{3},O_{4}\},

\displaystyle I=\{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4}\}\}.

The corresponding queries of
\tau(\mathbb{Q})
, under this choice of
\mathbb{C}
, would be

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle=P(\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}_{h_{1},h_{2},o_{1},o_{2},o_{3},o_{4}})=P(i_{m})

\displaystyle Q_{2}

\displaystyle=P(\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}_{h_{1},h_{2},o_{1},o_{2},o_{3},o_{4},g})=P(i_{m,g})

\displaystyle Q_{3}

\displaystyle=P(\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}_{a})=P(i_{a}).

If for some reason we decide to try to cluster
\mathbb{C}_{2}
further, such as by clustering
A,G,M
into a single variable
X
(Fig.
18(d)
), we violate condition C3, the answerability of the queries. How could we distinguish between queries like
P(I_{M=m}=i)
and
P(I_{A=a}=i)
if
M
and
A
are grouped in the same cluster? For this reason, we cannot proceed any further than
\mathbb{C}_{2}
, and, in fact,
\mathbb{C}_{2}
is actually the maximally answerable clusters from Lemma
9
.

\blacksquare

D.2
Discussion on the Abstract Invariance Condition

Recall the abstract invariance condition (AIC):
See
8

The AIC intuitively ensures that there is no loss of information when clustering two values together in the intravariable clusters
\mathbb{D}
. Specifically, it states that clustering two values together is safe whenever these values are interchangeable with respect to the behavior of downstream functions. That is, the functions are
invariant
to changes between values in the same intravariable cluster. This is illustrated in Fig.
20
. Examples
4
and
5
from Sec.
2
illustrate the subtleties of the AIC both when it holds and when it does not hold.

If the AIC is violated, then two functionally different values were placed in the same cluster. Hence, assuming that the AIC holds is reasonable provided that the intravariable clusters were chosen reasonably. For example, in a study where a drug
X
is reasonably expected to affect blood pressure levels
Y
, it would not make sense to cluster the values
X=0
(drug not taken) and
X=1
(drug taken) together since, by design, they have different downstream effects on
Y
and cannot be treated similarly.

On the other hand, in an image recognition setting, perhaps it does not matter for the task if the image is scaled or rotated, so these invariances can be modeled in the intravariable clustering. For example, if one image is a scaled version of another but they are otherwise the same, the two images might be placed in the same intravariable clustering without violating the AIC. Sec.
4
and Appendix
D.3
discuss this phenomenon in more detail.

If a data scientist finds the AIC too strict for her settings, one option is to revise the intravariable clusters, perhaps making them less coarse. Otherwise, the abstraction may be too strong and some important information is lost.

(a)
When the AIC holds, two values of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
that are in the same intravariable cluster are mapped to the same output in
\mathbf{V}_{H}
for any downstream function
f^{L}
after applying
\tau
.

(b)
When the AIC does not hold, two values of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
that are in the same intravariable cluster may map to different outputs in
\mathbf{V}_{H}
for some downstream function
f^{L}
after applying
\tau
.

Figure 20:
Visualization of the AIC (Def.
8
).

Still, there are alternatives if weaker assumptions are desired. The AIC ensures that an
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistent model exists, as demonstrated by Alg.
1
, but it is not a necessary condition in applications that do not require full
\mathcal{L}_{3}
-
\tau
consistency. For example, if one only works on the interventional level (
\mathcal{L}_{2}
), one may not necessarily care if consistencies between
\mathcal{L}_{3}
quantities are lost.

In the strictest form, making as minimal assumptions as needed for the task, a query-specific AIC can be assumed. That is,

25
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}
be a set of queries of the form
P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(\mathbf{y}_{H,1[\mathbf{x}_{H,1}]},\mathbf{y}_{H,2[\mathbf{x}_{H,2}]},\dots)
. For all
Q\in\mathbb{Q}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\forall i\mathbf{y}_{L,i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Y}_{L,i}}:\tau(\mathbf{y}_{L,i})=\mathbf{y}_{H,i}}\hskip-28.45274ptP^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(\mathbf{y}_{L,1[\mathbf{x}_{L,1}]},\mathbf{y}_{L,2[\mathbf{x}_{L,2}]},\dots)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\forall i\mathbf{y}_{L,i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Y}_{L,i}}:\tau(\mathbf{y}_{L,i})=\mathbf{y}_{H,i}}\hskip-28.45274ptP^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(\mathbf{y}_{L,1[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{L,1}]},\mathbf{y}_{L,2[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{L,2}]},\dots),

for all
\mathbf{x}_{L,i},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{L,i}
such that
\tau(\mathbf{x}_{L,i})=\tau(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{L,i})=\mathbf{x}_{H,i}
.

\blacksquare

In words,
\tau
satisfies Def.
25
if and only if there exists
\mathcal{M}_{H}
that is
Q
-
\tau
consistent with
\mathcal{M}_{L}
for all
Q\in\mathbb{Q}
, since all such variations of
Q
have the same probability. Still, it may be more useful to have a general criterion that is not query-dependent.

It turns out that the study of weaker forms of the AIC is not new and is sfirst discussed in
Chalupka, Perona, and Eberhardt 2015
. Broadly, the paper studies a similar abstraction setting in which causal macro-variables are learned from observational data from the low-level micro-variables. Similar to the intravariable learning problem posed in Sec.
4
, the paper aims to learn intravariable clusters of a low-level high-dimensional variable
I
(e.g., an image), which causes some other variable
T
, possibly with unobserved confounding. However, while the results of Sec.
4
focus on satisfying the AIC in Def.
8
,
Chalupka, Perona, and Eberhardt 2015
focuses on satisfying weaker conditions that are verifiable through data.

More specifically, consider the setting proposed in
Chalupka, Perona, and Eberhardt 2015
illustrated by the causal diagram in Fig.
21
. Note that Eq.
33
in Def.
8
requires that

\tau(f_{T}(i_{1},\mathbf{u}_{T}))=\tau(f_{T}(i_{2},\mathbf{u}_{T})),

(102)

for any
i_{1},i_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{I}
that are chosen to be clustered in the same intravariable cluster. This condition is a requirement on the SCM function
f_{T}
, which, as demonstrated by Lem.
5
, implies that one cannot cluster together any two values
i_{1},i_{2}
without further information about
f_{T}
, which may be difficult to acquire due to the unobserved nature of the SCM. Instead, the paper proposes two other properties. Notably, two values
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
are part of the same
observational partition

(
Chalupka, Perona, and Eberhardt 2015
, Def. 1)
if

P(t\mid i_{1})=P(t\mid i_{2}),

(103)

and they are part of the same
causal partition

(
Chalupka, Perona, and Eberhardt 2015
, Def. 3)
if

P(t_{i_{1}})=P(t_{i_{2}}).

(104)

Values of
\mathcal{D}_{I}
can easily be clustered according to Eq.
103
using the observational data. However, Eq.
104
may be a more desirable property if the goal of downstream tasks is to perform causal inferences from
I
to
T
.

It turns out that a clustering learned according to Eq.
103
typically also satisfies Eq.
104
according to a major proven result of the paper, the
Causal Coarsening Theorem

(
Chalupka, Perona, and Eberhardt 2015
, Thm. 5)
, which states (in informal terms) that the maximally coarse clustering that satisfies Eq.
104
is almost always a coarser clustering of the maximally coarse clustering that satisfies Eq.
103
. This essentially implies that, under certain faithfulness conditions, one can achieve the property of Eq.
104
simply as a consequence of achieving Eq.
103
, which can be accomplished with observational data alone.

The concepts of the partitions described by Eqs.
103
and
104
can be generalized to the general SCM/graph case. Suppose we are given the inter/intravariable clusters
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
. Consider a variant of the AIC applied on conditional probabilties:

26
Conditional Abstract Invariance Condition.

Let
P(\mathbf{V}_{L})
be an observational distribution over
\mathbf{V}_{L}
, and let
\tau:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{H}}
be a constructive abstraction function relative to
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
.
P(\mathbf{V}_{L})
is said to satisfy the conditional abstract invariance condition (conditional AIC, for short) w.r.t.
\tau
if, for all
\mathbf{X}_{L}
where
\mathbf{X}_{L}
is a union of clusters of
\mathbb{C}
, all
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}_{L}}
such that
\tau(\mathbf{x}_{1})=\tau(\mathbf{x}_{2})
, and all
\mathbf{v}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{H}}
, we have

\sum_{\mathbf{v}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}:\tau(\mathbf{v}_{L})=\mathbf{v}_{H}}\hskip-28.45274ptP(\mathbf{v}_{L}\mid\mathbf{x}_{1})=\sum_{\mathbf{v}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}:\tau(\mathbf{v}_{L})=\mathbf{v}_{H}}\hskip-28.45274ptP(\mathbf{v}_{L}\mid\mathbf{x}_{2}).

(105)

\blacksquare

I
T

Figure 21:
Problem setting in
Chalupka, Perona, and Eberhardt 2015
.

That is, two values can be clustered together as long as their conditional probabilities do not change in the space of
\mathbf{V}_{H}
. Note that this condition is entirely focused on the observational distributions
P(\mathbf{V}_{L})
and
P(\mathbf{V}_{H})
and make no requirements over any distributions from the higher layers,
\mathcal{L}_{2}
and
\mathcal{L}_{3}
.

Now consider an interventional variant:

27
Interventional Abstract Invariance Condition.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{L})
be a collection of interventional distributions over
\mathbf{V}_{L}
, and let
\tau:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{H}}
be a constructive abstraction function relative to
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
. We say that
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{L})
satisfies the interventional abstract invariance condition (interventional AIC, for short) w.r.t.
\tau
if, for all
\mathbf{X}_{L}
where
\mathbf{X}_{L}
is a union of clusters of
\mathbb{C}
, all
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}_{L}}
such that
\tau(\mathbf{x}_{1})=\tau(\mathbf{x}_{2})
, and all
\mathbf{v}_{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{H}}
, we have

\sum_{\mathbf{v}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}:\tau(\mathbf{v}_{L})=\mathbf{v}_{H}}\hskip-28.45274ptP(\mathbf{v}_{L[\mathbf{x}_{1}]})=\sum_{\mathbf{v}_{L}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}:\tau(\mathbf{v}_{L})=\mathbf{v}_{H}}\hskip-28.45274ptP(\mathbf{v}_{L[\mathbf{x}_{2}]}).

(106)

\blacksquare

This states that two values can be clustered together as long as the corresponding interventional distributions do not change in the space of
\mathbf{V}_{H}
.

These two definitions may be useful when looking to find abstractions while working on lower layers of the PCH. When given the C-DAG
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
, these can be refined to specific queries based on parent-child relationships in the graph. Although not shown formally here, it is likely that a more general variant of the Causal Coarsening Theorem could be proven for these two definitions. Nonetheless, using these definitions in place of the full AIC from Def.
8
has its limitations; for example, it may no longer be possible to perform counterfactual inferences on the high-level model. We encourage further research on this topic, comparing the relationships between variations of the AIC.

D.3
Representation Learning with Intravariable Clusters

Recall the following proposition from Sec.
4
:
See
5

When learning intravariable clusters, Prop.
5
states that without extra information, each value needs to be put in its own cluster. However, if extra information is available for use, this can be leveraged to improve the choice of
\mathbb{D}
. In practice, this is common, and is generally provided in the form of invariance assumptions. As a intuitive example, suppose that
X
is an image of an animal,
Y
is the corresponding label, and
f_{Y}(x,u_{Y})
describes the mechanism that humans use to classify
X
(here,
u_{Y}
is the exogenous noise). Suppose that
g(x,\phi)
is a function that rotates
x
by
\phi
degrees. It is the case that the classification of animals is rotationally invariant, that is,
f_{Y}(g(x,\phi),u_{Y})=f_{Y}(x,u_{Y})
for all
x
,
\phi
,
u_{Y}
. Then, in fact,
\mathbb{D}
can be defined such that values of
x_{1},x_{2}
are clustered together if
g(x_{1},\phi)=x_{2}
, as shown by the following result.

12
.

For each
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
, suppose that there exists function
g_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\times\mathcal{D}_{\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
(with parameters
\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
) such that

f^{L}_{V}(\mathbf{c}_{i},\mathbf{u}_{V})=f^{L}_{V}(g_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\mathbf{c}_{i},\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}),\mathbf{u}_{V})

(107)

for all
V
that are children of
\mathbf{C}_{i}
and all
\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\in\mathcal{D}_{\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}}
. Then,
\mathcal{M}_{L}
satisfies the AIC w.r.t. any constructive abstraction function with
\mathbb{D}
such that
\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(1)},\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(2)}
being in the same intravariable cluster implies that
g_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(1)},\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}})=\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(2)}
for some
\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
.

\blacksquare

Proof.

Consider the proposed set of intravariable clusters
\mathbb{D}
. Let
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}
be two values such that
\tau(\mathbf{v}_{1})=\tau(\mathbf{v}_{2})
. The goal is to show that for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{L}}
and
\mathbf{C}_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\begin{split}&\tau\left(\left(f^{L}_{V}(\mathbf{pa}_{V}^{(1)},\mathbf{u}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{k}\right)\right)\\
&=\tau\left(\left(f^{L}_{V}(\mathbf{pa}_{V}^{(2)},\mathbf{u}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{k}\right)\right),\end{split}

(108)

where
\mathbf{pa}_{V}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{pa}_{V}^{(2)}
are the values corresponding to
\mathbf{v}_{1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{2}
respectively. If
\tau(\mathbf{v}_{1})=\tau(\mathbf{v}_{2})
, that implies that for all
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
(where
\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(2)}
correspond to the values of
\mathbf{v}_{1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{2}
respectively),
\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(2)}
must be in the same intravariable cluster in
\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
(this includes the case where
\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(1)}=\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(2)}
). By construction of
\mathbb{D}
, this is only possible if
g_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(1)},\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}})=\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(2)}
for some
\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
.

For
g_{V}=\{g_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}:\mathbf{C}_{i}\cap\mathbf{Pa}_{V}\neq\emptyset\}
, denote
g_{V}(\mathbf{pa}_{V}^{(1)},\phi)
as the collection of outputs of
g_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\mathbf{c}_{i}^{(1)},\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}})
for the functions of
g_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\in g_{V}
, which, as established earlier, is equal to
\mathbf{pa}_{V}^{(2)}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\tau\left(\left(f^{L}_{V}(\mathbf{pa}_{V}^{(1)},\mathbf{u}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{k}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\tau\left(\left(f^{L}_{V}(g_{V}(\mathbf{pa}_{V}^{(1)},\phi),\mathbf{u}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{k}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\tau\left(\left(f^{L}_{V}(\mathbf{pa}_{V}^{(2)},\mathbf{u}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{k}\right)\right),

completing the proof.
∎

For intuition, consider the following simple example.

17
.

Consider a situation where
Y
is some binary variable that is a (noisy) bitwise AND of two other binary variables
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
. For example, perhaps
Y
denotes whether a law is enacted, and
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
denotes the votes of the two branches of government, like in Ex.
11
. Formally, let
\mathcal{M}_{L}
be defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{L}

\displaystyle=\{U_{X_{1}},U_{X_{2}},U_{Y}\},\text{ all binary}

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{L}

\displaystyle=\{X_{1},X_{2},Y\},\text{ all binary}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{L}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}X_{1}\leftarrow f^{L}_{X_{1}}(u_{X_{1}})&=u_{X_{1}}\\
X_{2}\leftarrow f^{L}_{X_{2}}(u_{X_{2}})&=u_{X_{2}}\\
Y\leftarrow f^{L}_{Y}(x_{1},x_{2},u_{Y})&=(x_{1}\wedge x_{2})\oplus u_{Y}\end{cases}

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{U}_{L})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}P(U_{X_{1}}=1)&=P(U_{X_{2}}=1)=0.5\\
P(U_{Y}=1)&=0.1\end{cases}

Now suppose we want to create an abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
using the intervariable clusters
\mathbb{C}=\{\mathbf{C}_{1}=\{X_{1},X_{2}\},\mathbf{C}_{2}=\{Y\}\}
. The domain
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{1}}
has four values as
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
can both be either 0 or 1. However, as emphasized by Prop.
5
, the only intravariable clustering we can choose without additional information is the one where each value is in its own cluster. In other words,
\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{1}}=\{\{(X_{1}=0,X_{2}=0)\},\{(X_{1}=0,X_{2}=1)\},\{(X_{1}=1,X_{2}=0)\},\{(X_{1}=1,X_{2}=1)\}\}
.

However, suppose we are given additional information that
f_{Y}
is
permutation invariant
to its endogenous inputs, i.e.,

f_{Y}(x_{1},x_{2},u_{Y})=f_{Y}(x_{2},x_{1},u_{Y}).

(109)

In the notation of Prop.
12
, we can define
\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{1}}
to be a binary variable such that 0 means original order and 1 means reversed. Then we can define
g(x_{1},x_{2},\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{1}})=(x_{1},x_{2})
if
\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{1}}=0
or
(x_{2},x_{1})
if
\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{1}}=1
. Then, this implies that
f_{Y}(x_{1},x_{2},u_{Y})=f_{Y}(g(x_{1},x_{2},\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{1}}),u_{Y})
for any choice of
\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{1}}
.

By Prop.
12
, this implies that
(X_{1}=0,X_{2}=1)
and
(X_{1}=1,X_{2}=0)
can be placed in the same intravariable cluster without violating the AIC. Indeed, the function
f^{L}_{Y}(x_{1},x_{2},u_{Y})=(x_{1}\wedge x_{2})\oplus u_{Y}
does not change when
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are swapped, so we have

\begin{split}&\tau\left(\left(f^{L}_{Y}(X_{1}=0,X_{2}=1,\mathbf{u}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{k}\right)\right)\\
&=\tau\left(\left(f^{L}_{Y}(X_{1}=1,X_{2}=0,\mathbf{u}_{V}):V\in\mathbf{C}_{k}\right)\right),\end{split}

confirming that the AIC still holds when
(X_{1}=0,X_{2}=1)
and
(X_{1}=1,X_{2}=0)
can be placed in the same intravariable cluster.

\blacksquare

In practice, this invariance information can be incorporated in the process of learning the intravariable clusters while training
\widehat{\tau}
in an RNCM, as described in Def.
11
. To take into account Prop.
12
, the training objective could include a term to enforce the invariance specified by
g_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
(e.g. through a penalty on
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\mathbf{c}_{i},\bm{\theta}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}})-\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(g_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\mathbf{c}_{i},\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}),\bm{\theta}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}})
).

In fact, it turns out that the idea of intravariable clusters works in tandem with techniques in deep representation learning when it comes to incorporating invariances in the data. In the case of image data for example, many works in computer vision have already leveraged general patterns found in images (e.g. rotations, crops, flips, etc. do not affect classification) to achieve faster training with less data
(
Shorten and Khoshgoftaar 2019
)
. Permutation invariance concepts
(
Zaheer et al. 2017
;
Murphy et al. 2019
)
have been used as pooling functions for convolutional neural networks
(
LeCun and Bengio 1998
)
. Existing frameworks for representation learning such as through contrastive methods
(
Chen et al. 2020
)
can be used for learning
\tau
.

Appendix E
Additional Examples

This section contains examples to improve the clarity of concepts in the paper.

E.1
Sec.
2
Examples

We provide more examples of the concepts in Sec.
2
, namely, abstractions constructed through inter/intravariable clusters. In addition to Example
1
within the section, we provide a more involved example below.

18
.

Suppose an economist is studying the effects of implementing a new type of government policy on recession prevention. The economist records data on several variables of interest: whether the policy is implemented (
X
); whether the policy is lobbied (
W
); economic spending in terms of consumption (
C
), investment (
I
), government spending (
G
), imports (
M
), and exports (
E
); and whether or not there is a recession (
Y
). Out of these variables,
Z
,
X
, and
Y
are binary, and
C
,
I
,
G
,
M
, and
E
are numerical values representing how much the spending has .changed relative to the previous year (in billions of dollars).

Suppose the true SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}=\mathcal{M}_{L}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{L}

\displaystyle=\{U_{W},U_{X},U_{XY},U_{C},U_{I},U_{G},U_{M},U_{E},U_{IG},

\displaystyle U_{CM},U_{EM}\}

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{L}

\displaystyle=\{W,X,C,I,G,M,E,Y\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{L}

\displaystyle=\{

\displaystyle W\leftarrow f_{W}(u_{W})=u_{W}

\displaystyle X\leftarrow f_{X}(w,u_{X},u_{XY})=w\oplus u_{X}\oplus u_{XY}

\displaystyle C\leftarrow f_{C}(x,g,u_{C})=5x-0.2g+u_{C}+u_{CM}

\displaystyle I\leftarrow f_{I}(x,u_{I})=-5x+u_{I}+u_{IG}

\displaystyle G\leftarrow f_{G}(x,u_{G})=10x+u_{G}+u_{IG}

\displaystyle E\leftarrow f_{E}(u_{E})=u_{E}+u_{EM}

\displaystyle M\leftarrow f_{M}(c,u_{M})=0.2c+u_{M}+u_{CM}-u_{EM}

\displaystyle Y\leftarrow f_{Y}(c,i,g,e,m,u_{XY})=

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{1}\{c+i+g+e-m\leq 0\}\oplus u_{XY}

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{U}_{L})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}U_{W},U_{X}&\sim\bern(0.5)\\
U_{XY}&\sim\bern(0.1)\\
U_{C},U_{I},U_{G},U_{M},U_{E}&\sim N(0,10)\\
U_{IG},U_{CM},U_{EM}&\sim N(0,2)\end{cases}

To summarize, the policy has some impact on the consumption, investment, and government spending. Whether or not there is a recession depends on imports and exports in addition to all of these factors. Indeed, despite the fact that this is a toy example, the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is already quite complex to describe with this many variables and functions, and perhaps this level of detail is not needed to achieve the inference we desire. We will see how utilizing abstractions can help with this.

We first note that perhaps
\mathbf{V}_{L}
contains too many micro-level variables that can be summarized with a smaller set of abstract higher-level variables. Further, perhaps using real-valued variables is overly complex, and the same phenomena can be described without loss of generality using a lower-dimensional space. For entertaining these considerations, we utilize the idea of intervariable and intravariable clusters.

Suppose we are only interested in the causal effect of the policy
X
on recession
Y
. We could study the same phenomenon under a simpler set of variables. To do so, we can cluster variables of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
to form a new set of macro-level variables. First, note that although we may have data on
C
,
I
,
G
,
E
, and
M
, it may make more sense to simply cluster them together and consider them as one variable (e.g. GDP). Further, perhaps we may decide that
W
is not relevant to the analysis and exclude it from the study. We leave these clustering decisions at the discretion of the data scientist (e.g. the economist in this example).

By the definition of intervariable clusters from Def.
5
, we can choose clusters
\mathbb{C}=\{\mathbf{C}_{1}=\{X\},\mathbf{C}_{2}=\{C,I,G,E,M\},\mathbf{C}_{3}=\{Y\}\}
. We leave
X
and
Y
in their own clusters while grouping all of
C
,
I
,
G
,
E
,
M
into one cluster
\mathbf{C}_{2}
.
W
is excluded from all of the clusters and is effectively projected out of the system. We can then treat
\mathbf{C}_{1}
,
\mathbf{C}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{C}_{3}
as our new variables. Let us relabel them
X
,
Z
, and
Y
respectively, where
Z
. We can define our higher level variables as
\mathbf{V}_{H}=\{X,Z,Y\}
.

Further, it is not immediately clear what the domain of
Z
is. Certainly, it could be left as a tuple
(C,I,G,E,M)\in\mathbb{R}^{5}
. However, this would be an overly complex representation of
Z
, and we do not need to retain all of the joint information of
C,I,G,E,M
. In other words,
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
does not have to be equal to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}
and can be represented more compactly. Instead, we may choose to define
Z
as the GDP, or
Z=C+I+G-E-M
. This can be described through the use of intravariable clusters.

By the definition of intravariable clusters from Def.
5
, we must choose
\mathbb{D}=\{\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{1}},\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}},\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{3}}\}
, where
\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
is a partitioning of the domain
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
. Since
X
and
Y
are already binary variables, we will not be able to group their values together, so we simply define
\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{1}}
and
\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}
as
\{\{0\},\{1\}\}
. For
\mathbf{C}_{2}
however, we must cluster values of
(C,I,G,E,M)
such that
(c_{1},i_{1},g_{1},e_{1},m_{1}),(c_{2},i_{2},g_{2},e_{2},m_{2})\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}
are in the same cluster if and only if
c_{1}+i_{1}+g_{1}+e_{1}-m_{1}=c_{2}+i_{2}+g_{2}+e_{2}-m_{2}
. In other words, we can, for example, define
\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}=\{\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}^{j}:(c,i,g,e,m)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}},c+i+g+e-m=j\}
.

Now, with the intravariable clusters
\mathbb{D}
defined, we can choose the domains of
\mathbf{V}_{H}
as simply their corresponding clusters in
\mathbb{D}
. That is, each value of
Z
corresponds to some
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}^{j}
. In fact, we can simply set
Z=j
, where
j=C+I+G+E-M
, which intuitively corresponds to the idea that
Z
represents the annual change in GDP. Note that the domain of
Z
,
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
becomes smaller than
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}
in some sense. First, it is lower dimensional (
\mathbb{R}
instead of
\mathbb{R}^{5}
), and second, there are clearly values of
(C,I,G,E,M)
that are mapped to the same value of
Z
. For example,
(C=1,I=2,G=3,E=4,M=2
maps to the same value as
(C=3,I=2,G=1,E=5,M=3)
because
1+2+3+4-2=3+2+1+5-3=8
.

However, perhaps this level of dimensionality reduction is insufficient. In terms of cardinality,
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
is the same size as
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}
as the cardinality of
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{5}
are the same. Perhaps the domain of
Z
could be compressed further. Indeed, we could turn
Z
into a binary variable by defining
\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}=\{\mathcal{D}^{0}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}},\mathcal{D}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}\}
, where
(c,i,g,e,m)\in\mathcal{D}^{0}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}
if
c+i+g+e-m>0
or it is in
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}
otherwise. In other words, we drop all information about the annual change in GDP except whether it is positive or negative. We will use this clustering for the rest of the examples, and we will see in later examples why this clustering is allowed and makes sense. Sec.
4
expands on the general discussion of which intravariable cluster choices are allowed.

With the idea of inter/intravariable clusters, the concept of constructive abstraction functions can be established as in Def.
6
. From Example
18
, the function
\tau
constructed from
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
is clear. For example, consider the value
\mathbf{v}_{L}=(W=0,X=1,C=2.6,I=-1.2,G=10.2,E=0.4,M=1.2,Y=0)
. We can compute
\mathbf{v}_{H}=\tau(\mathbf{v}_{L})
and find that
\mathbf{v}_{H}=(X=1,Z=0,Y=0)
. In this case,
\tau=(\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{1}},\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{2}},\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{3}})
, where
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{1}}
and
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{3}}
are the identity function on
X
and
Y
respectively, and
\tau_{\mathbf{C}_{2}}(C=2.6,I=-1.2,G=10.2,E=0.4,M=1.2)=\mathbf{1}\{2.6-1.2+10.2+0.4-1.2\leq 0\}=0
. That is, for
\mathbf{C}_{2}
, the intravariable clusters are defined such that values of
(c,i,g,e,m)
are divided into two categories depending on whether
c+i+g+e-m\leq 0
, which we can arbitrarily choose as the binary values
0
and
1
. This provides a mapping from every value of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{L}}
to some value of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}_{H}}
.

\blacksquare

Note that
\tau
alone does not define an abstraction. While
\tau
provides a well-defined mapping from
\mathbf{V}_{L}
to
\mathbf{V}_{H}
, not every SCM defined over
\mathbf{V}_{H}
can be considered an abstraction of
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. Consider the following example.

19
.

Consider the SCM
\mathcal{M}_{H}
defined as follows:

\mathcal{M}_{H}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{U}_{H}&=\{U_{X},U_{Z},U_{Y}\}\\
\mathbf{V}_{H}&=\{X,Z,Y\}\\
\mathcal{F}_{H}&=\begin{cases}X\leftarrow f_{X}(u_{X})&=u_{X}\\
Z\leftarrow f_{Z}(u_{Z})&=u_{Z}\\
Y\leftarrow f_{Y}(u_{Y})&=u_{Y}\end{cases}\\
P(\mathbf{U}_{H})&=U_{X},U_{Z},U_{Y}\sim\bern(0.5)\end{cases}

One could argue that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is defined over
\mathbf{V}_{H}
, constructed via
\tau
from Example
18
. However, it is trivial to see that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
retains none of the meaning of the variables of
\mathbf{V}_{H}
intended by
\tau
and is clearly oblivious of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
. After all, there is not even any causal relationship between the variables defined in
\mathcal{M}_{H}
. Intuitively,
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is clearly not an abstraction of
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, and this is reinforced by the fact that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
does not match common definitions of abstractions, such as Def.
15
.

However, assuming that we do not have access to
\mathcal{M}_{H}
or
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, and can only observe them through their distributions of the PCH, how could we tell that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is not an abstraction of
\mathcal{M}^{*}
? Certainly, there are inconsistencies in the distribution too. For example, note that

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(C+I+G+E-M>0\mid X=1)

\displaystyle=P(5-0.2g+u_{C}+u_{CM}-5+u_{I}+u_{IG}+10+u_{G}

\displaystyle+u_{IG}+u_{E}+u_{EM}-0.2c-u_{M}-u_{CM}-u_{EM}>0)

\displaystyle=P(5-0.2(10+u_{G}+u_{IG})+u_{C}+u_{CM}-5+u_{I}

\displaystyle+u_{IG}+10+u_{G}+u_{IG}+u_{E}+u_{EM}

\displaystyle-0.2(5-0.2(10+u_{G}+u_{IG})+u_{C}+u_{CM})

\displaystyle-u_{M}-u_{CM}-u_{EM}>0)

\displaystyle=P(7.4+0.84u_{G}+1.8u_{IG}+0.8u_{C}-0.2u_{CM}

\displaystyle+1.04u_{I}+u_{E}-u_{M}>0)

\displaystyle=P(N(0,7.056)+N(0,6.48)+N(0,6.4)+N(0,0.08)

\displaystyle+N(0,10.816)+N(0,10)+N(0,10)>-7.4)

\displaystyle=P(N(0,50.832)>-7.4)\approx 0.85.

On the other hand, we see that
P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(Z=0\mid X=1)=P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(Z=0)=P(U_{Z}=0)=0.5
. We would expect that if
\mathcal{M}_{H}
were an abstraction of
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, then
P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(Z=0\mid X=1)
should match
P^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(C+I+G+E-M>0\mid X=1)
, which is evidently not the case.

The intuition behind this connection is that
\tau
directly maps cases of
C+I+G+E-M>0
to cases of
Z=0
, so their corresponding probabilities should be the same. If
Q=P^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(C+I+G+E-M>0\mid X=1)
, we would say that
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is not
Q
-
\tau
consistent with
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, via Def.
7
.

\blacksquare

Not all functions mapping between two spaces of variables are constructive abstraction functions. Some abstraction functions, while not being constructive, may still have qualities that are expected from abstractions. However, the lack of structure in these functions make certain features of the abstraction models difficult to define, such as the concept of
Q
-
\tau
consistency. The following example describes a few such cases.

20
.

To start, we note that when fixing the intervariable and intravariable clusters
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
, the corresponding constructive abstraction function
\tau
is deterministic, and the corresponding
\mathbf{V}_{H}
is fixed, up to a bijective mapping of its domain. For example, suppose
\mathbb{C}=\{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}\}
, and
\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{A}}=\{\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{2}\},\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{B}}=\{\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{2}\},\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{C}}=\{\mathbf{c}_{1},\mathbf{c}_{2}\}
. However, let
\mathbf{V}_{H}=\{X,Y,Z,W\}
, where
\mathcal{D}_{X}=\mathcal{D}_{Y}=\mathcal{D}_{Z}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathcal{D}_{W}=\{0,1,2\}
. While a function could be constructed from
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{C}
, it could not be a valid mapping to
\mathbf{V}_{H}
. First of all, there are four variables in
\mathbf{V}_{H}
but only three clusters in
\mathbb{C}
, so the mapping could not be one-to-one.

Even if
X
were removed, all of the intervariable clusters have values clustered into two sets, but
W
is a ternary variable, so a one-to-one mapping is not possible with any of the intervariable clusters. However, if
W
were removed from
\mathbf{V}_{H}
, then
\tau
could be a valid constructive abstraction mapping between
\mathbf{V}_{L}
and
\mathbf{V}_{H}
, since the other three variables are binary. The number of variables of
\mathbf{V}_{H}
and their domain sizes match the number of clusters of
\mathbb{C}
and the number within each cluster of
\mathbb{D}
, respectively. In fact, at least without considering the model distributions, any cluster of
\mathbb{C}
could map to any of the variables of
\mathbf{V}_{H}\setminus\{W\}
as they are isomorphic.

In other words,
\tau
fails to be a constructive tau abstraction for any choice of
\mathbf{V}_{H}
that is incompatible with
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
. Intuitively, this means that when given
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
, the function
\tau
and high level variables
\mathbf{V}_{H}
are already fixed, which is how the rest of this paper is framed. However, there can exist cases with some function
\tau
mapping from
\mathbf{V}_{L}
to
\mathbf{V}_{H}
such that
\tau
cannot be considered a constructive abstraction function for any choice of
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
. Some cases may even appear to be valid abstractions intuitively.

Consider an example of a company board setting. Suppose in a company, the board consists of the CEO, Alice (
A
), and two vice presidents, Bob (
B
) and Charlie (
C
). When voting on company policies, each board member can choose to vote for (
+1
), vote against (
-1
), or abstain (
0
). In other words,
A
,
B
, and
C
are ternary variables with domain
\{-1,0,+1\}
. While data can be collected on each of the members’ voting behaviors (
V_{L}=\{A,B,C\}
), it may be more sensible to aggregate the votes into a more useful quantity. Suppose two high level variables are computed (
\mathbf{V}_{H}=\{X,Z\}
) through some abstraction function
\tau=(\tau_{X},\tau_{Z})
, defined as follows.

\displaystyle\tau_{X}(a,b,c)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}+1&a+b+c>0\\
0&a+b+c=0\\
-1&a+b+c<0\end{cases}

\displaystyle\tau_{Z}(a,b,c)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}a&a\neq 0\\
+1&a=0,b+c>0\\
0&a=0,b+c=0\\
-1&a=0,b+c<0\end{cases}

In words,
X
is the aggregate vote that is simply the majority vote of all three members. On the other hand
Z
is an aggregate vote that prioritizes Alice’s vote, as she is CEO. If she abstains, then it is an aggregate of the votes of the vice presidents. For example,
\tau(A=+1,B=-1,C=-1)=(X=-1,Z=+1)
.

Perhaps in company matters, it is more useful to use the variables
X
and
Z
over the individual votes of the board members. However,
\tau
cannot be considered a constructive abstraction function for any choice of clusters
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
. This is because both
X
and
Z
change values depending on all of the values of
A
,
B
, and
C
, so the variables of
\mathbf{V}_{L}
cannot be cleanly separated into two different clusters.

Even so, this choice of
\tau
seems like it could result in a valid abstraction. The difficulty lies with the analysis of causal quantities after
\tau
is fixed. Without the notion of clusters, the definition of
Q
-
\tau
consistency fails to work, so it is no longer clear what causal quantities correspond to what. For example, what would an intervention of
A=+1
imply on the
\mathbf{V}_{H}
level? Or, what would an intervention on
Z=+1
imply on the
\mathbf{V}_{L}
level? The answer might change depending on the setting, or there may not even be an answer that makes sense at all. This is why prior works like
Rubenstein et al. 2017
define the mapping between interventions separately from
\tau
, which maps the variables.

In this particular case, provided that queries do not require separation of
X
and
Z
(e.g. queries like
P(X_{Z=z}=x)
are not needed), then it may be sensible to cluster
A
,
B
, and
C
together into one intervariable cluster and then simply have one variable in
\mathbf{V}_{H}
used for downstream tasks. This allows for the theory in this paper to be applied, significantly reducing the complexity of defining the abstractions.

\blacksquare

See the following for a negative example of an abstraction of the drug example in Ex.
3
.

21
.

Continuing Example
3
, consider the following SCM
\mathcal{M}_{H}
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{H}

\displaystyle=\{U_{X},U_{Y}\}

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{H}

\displaystyle=\{X,Y\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{H}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}X\leftarrow f^{H}_{X}(u_{X})&=u_{X}\\
Y\leftarrow f^{H}_{Y}(u_{Y})&=u_{Y}\end{cases}

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{U}_{H})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}P(U_{X}=1)&=0.5\\
P(U_{Y}=1)&=0.2\end{cases}

Indeed, this choice of
\mathcal{M}_{H}
is defined over
X
and
Y
. However, it does not seem like there is any connection between
\mathcal{M}_{H}
and
\mathcal{M}_{L}
from Example
3
, even if
\mathbf{V}_{H}=\tau(\mathbf{V}_{L})
. To verify this, we can compare the distributions induced by the two models. Note that while
P^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(Y=1\mid A=1,B=1)\approx 0.853
, we see that
P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(Y=1\mid X=1)=P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(Y=1)=P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(U_{Y}=1)=0.2
. It seems that these two quantities should be related, as
\tau
maps
(A=1,B=1)
to
X=1
, yet they are clearly not equal in the two models. This is similar for the causal effect
P^{\mathcal{M}_{L}}(Y_{A=1,B=1}=1)
. Computing
P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(Y_{X=1}=1)=P^{\mathcal{M}_{H}}(Y=1)=0.2
actually yields the same result, which is clearly incorrect. In fact, it even seems that the causal relations are incorrect, as
f^{H}_{Y}
does not use
X
as an input.

\blacksquare

Example
21
shows an example of
\mathcal{M}_{H}
that is a poor abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
despite the fact that it is defined over the space of
\mathbf{V}_{H}
mapped by
\tau
. From the example, intuition tells us that a proper abstraction of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
should match
\mathcal{M}_{L}
in certain quantities, including observational, interventional, and counterfactual quantities. Specifically, there are quantities induced by
\mathcal{M}_{L}
that appear to have matching counterparts in
\mathcal{M}_{H}
based on
\tau
. This notion is made concrete through the concept of
Q
-
\tau
consistency (Def.
7
).
